4
Experiments

We evaluate our FDE methods with baselines through two experiments: linear quadratic regulator (LQR) and Atari games. LQR is a parametric setting widely studied in RL literature
[
8
,
61
,
35
, e.g.,]
. For the LQR experiment, the conclusion from the error comparison of the FDE methods (PDFL2, RBF, Matern, Energy, KL) aligns with the convergence rates in Table
2
, and the FDE methods outperformed the baseline FLE
[
61
]
. Due to space limitations, we defer the details to Appendix
E.1
.

Atari games are common testbeds for distributional RL methods
[
4
,
14
,
38
,
49
,
63
, e.g.,]
.
We compared the proposed FDE methods with FLE
[
61
]
, QRDQN
[
14
]
and IQN
[
13
]
). Among these methods, QRDQN and IQN rely on quantile-based modeling, and we adopted Gaussian mixture modeling (GMM, see details in Appendix
E.2.1
) to model distributions for all other methods.
In our experiments, we tested different numbers of mixtures / quantiles (
M=10,100,200
) and considered multiple Atari games: Atlantis, Breakout, Enduro, KunfuMaster, Pong, Qbert, SpaceInvader.
We estimated
\Upsilon_{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
with the target policy being the epsilon-greedy variant (e.g., see Section 2.2 of
[
50
]
) of the DQN-trained policy
\pi^{*}

[
33
]
, namely
\pi=\pi_{{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}}^{*}
with epsilon level
{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}=0.3
. We collected offline data through the trajectory of an agent following a behavior policy
b=\pi_{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}^{*}
with
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.4
.
See Appendix
E.2.2
for other algorithmic details.

In our simulations, we have included the following methods: (i) baseline methods (FLE
[
61
]
, QRDQN, IQN), (ii) our FDE methods (KL, Energy, PDF-L2, RBF), (iii) non-functional Bregman divergence (TVD)
that we did not study. Figure
1
, which contains these methods in order from left to right for each game,
shows the inaccuracy comparison with
N=10K
and
M=200
.
Although the performance of our FDE methods does not perfectly align with the comparison of convergence rates shown in Table
2
(presumably due to violation of underlying assumptions (e.g., Assumption
3.1
) and optimization challenges in deep neural networks (e.g., local minima)), our FDE methods outperform the baseline methods in most games with respect to mean inaccuracy and variance. Besides, TVD malfunctions, which corroborates
our proposal to build the objective functions based on functional Bregman divergence in Section
2.4
.

We have also conducted more simulations under different settings for all seven games (see Figures
3
–
5
of Appendix
E.2.4
) and tried with different sample sizes
N=2K,5K,10K
(see Tables
5
–
32
). These contain (a) the case where reward is random, and (b) different behavior policies that lead to worse coverage (Assumption
C.3
). Both lead to similar conclusions as before.
We also demonstrated that another functional Bregman divergence—the Hyvärinen divergence, which was not part of our main study—performs well in practice, supporting our discussion in Section
2.4
.

Figure 1:
Mean (dots) and confidence region (
\text{Mean}\pm\text{STD}
) for 5 seeds based on
N=10K
samples:
\mathbb{W}_{1}
-inaccuracy (Y-axis) for each method (X-axis) for the games with
M=200
. See Figure
3
–
5
for simulation results in all seven games.

5
Discussion

We acknowledge several key limitations of our study. First, while our framework establishes the sufficiency of using functional Bregman divergence to ensure the property (
9
) (Theorem
2.4
), the necessity remains an open question.
Second, for simplicity, our theoretical analysis assumes data splitting, leading to independent samples at each iteration. It would be valuable to extend the analysis to scenarios where data are reused across iterations.
Finally, our theorems rely on the completeness assumption (Assumption
3.1
), which may be overly restrictive in practical settings. We could introduce a term that quantifies violations of this assumption via the inherent Bellman error
[
36
]
and incorporate it into our final bound. We leave such an extension for future work.

Appendix A
Definitions of metrics and divergences

Following is the generalized algorithm of FDE methods.

Algorithm 1

Fitted distributional evaluation

Input:

Functional Bregman divergence
\mathfrak{d}
(Tables
1
–
2
), Model
\mathcal{M}
, Initial
\Upsilon_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
, Data
\mathcal{D}

Output:

\Upsilon_{T}

for

t=1

to

T

do

Perform the minimization (
5
) to obtain
\Upsilon_{t}
.

end

for

For Tables
1
and
2
of Section
3
, we shall define the necessary concepts needed to understand the objective functions and contractive metrics.

A.1
Contractive metrics

Following are the individual metrics that satisfy (S-L-C) and their supremum (
7
) and expectation-extensions (
8
) (which are contractive metrics (Definition
2.2
)) that we used in Tables
1
and
2
.

Wasserstein-p metric is defined as follows, with
J(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
being the possible joint distributions of
\mathbf{X}\sim\mu_{1}
and
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mu_{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf_{J(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y}\|^{p}\bigg)^{1/p}\quad(p\geq 1).

Its supremum and expectation extensions are defined as follows, with their contractive factors (
\zeta
of Definition
2.2
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{p,\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{W}_{p}(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a))\quad\text{with }\zeta=\gamma,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big\{\mathbb{W}_{p}^{2p}(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a))\Big\}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2p}}\quad\text{with }\zeta=\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}.

MMD-Energy is defined as the square-rooted form of following MMD-squared (with
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
all being indepenent)

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{\beta}(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{\beta}(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{\beta}(X,Y)\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad k_{\beta}(x,y):=\|x\|^{\beta}+\|y\|^{\beta}-\|x-y\|^{\beta}\quad(0<\beta<2).

Its supremum and expectation extensions are defined as follows, with

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\beta,\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\sup_{s,a}{\rm MMD}_{\beta}(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a))\quad(0<\beta<2)\quad\text{with }\zeta=\gamma^{\frac{\beta}{2}},

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm MMD}}_{\beta,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{s,a\sim d_{\pi}}\Big\{{\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s.a)\Big\}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}(1<\beta<2)\text{ with }\zeta=\gamma^{\frac{\beta-1}{2}}.

A.2
Divergences

Functional Bregman divergences (choices suggested in Tables
1
and
2
) are what determine the objective functions and their corresponding choices of
\mathcal{P}
, whose elements can be used as inputs of
\mathfrak{d}
. Our model should satisfy
{\mathcal{M}_{\Theta}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
.

For Cramer FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}=l_{2}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
:

\displaystyle l_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\int_{b_{1}}^{b_{2}}\Big|F(z|\mu_{1})-F(z|\mu_{2})\Big|^{2}\mathrm{d}z\quad\text{with }F(\cdot|\mu)\text{ being cdf of }\mu\in\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\Delta([b_{1},b_{2}]):=\{\text{probability measures of distributions bounded in }[b_{1},b_{2}]\subsetneq\mathbb{R}\}.

For Energy FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
(
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
being mutually independent):

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{\beta}(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{\beta}(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{\beta}(X,Y)\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad k_{\beta}(x,y):=\|x\|^{\beta}+\|y\|^{\beta}-\|x-y\|^{\beta}\quad(0<\beta<2),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ (d\geq 1):\|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}\{k_{\beta}(X,\cdot)\}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\}\quad\text{with }\mathcal{H}_{\beta}\ \text{being the RKHS of kernel }k_{\beta}.

For PDF-L2 FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}=d_{L_{2}}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
:

\displaystyle d_{L_{2}}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|f(z|\mu_{1})-f(z|\mu_{2})\Big|^{2}\mathrm{d}z\quad\text{with }f(\cdot|\mu)\text{ being pdf of }\mu\in\mathcal{P},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\bigg\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ (d\geq 1):\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f^{2}(z|\mu)\mathrm{d}z<\infty\bigg\}.

For MMD-Matern FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\nu}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
(
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
being mutually independent):

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\nu}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{\nu}(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{\nu}(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{\nu}(X,Y)\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad k_{\nu}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\frac{2^{1-\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu)}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\nu}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|}{{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}}\bigg)^{\nu}\cdot K_{\nu}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\nu}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|}{{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}}\bigg)\text{ for some }{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}>0,\nu>0,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ (d\geq 1):\|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}\{k_{\nu}(X,\cdot)\}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\}\quad\text{with }\mathcal{H}_{\nu}\ \text{being the RKHS of kernel }k_{\nu}.

For MMD-RBF FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
(
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
being mutually independent):

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}(X,Y)\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{(2\sqrt{\pi})^{d}}\cdot{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{-d}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-1}{4{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}\bigg),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ (d\geq 1):\|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}\{k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}(X,\cdot)\}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\}\ \text{with }\mathcal{H}_{{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}}\ \text{being the RKHS of }k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}.

For MMD-RBF-transformed FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{(\sigma,f)}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
(with conditions of function
f
and definition of
I_{f}
are mentioned in Equations 8 and 10 of
[
68
]
) (
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
being mutually independent):

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{(\sigma,f)}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{(\sigma,f)}(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{(\sigma,f)}(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{(\sigma,f)}(X,Y)\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad k_{(\sigma,f)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\exp\bigg(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)\cdot I_{f}\bigg(\frac{\|\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}\|}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg),

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ (d\geq 1):\|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}\{k_{(\sigma,f)}(X,\cdot)\}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\}\ \text{with }\mathcal{H}_{(\sigma,f)}\ \text{being the RKHS of }k_{(\sigma,f)}.

For MMD-Coulomb FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{cou}^{2}
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
(
X,X^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
being mutually independent):

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{cou}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{cou}(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{cou}(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{cou}(X,Y)\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad k_{cou}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\kappa_{0}^{(cou)}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}):=\begin{cases}-\log\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|&\text{if}\quad d=2\\
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2-d}&\text{if}\quad d\geq 3\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ (d\geq 2):\|\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}\{k_{cou}(X,\cdot)\}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\}\ \text{with }\mathcal{H}_{cou}\ \text{being the RKHS of }k_{cou}.

For KL FDE, we use
\mathfrak{d}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\text{KL}(\mu_{2},\mu_{1})
and corresponding
\mathcal{P}
:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mu_{2}\|\mu_{1}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z|\mu_{2})\cdot\log\frac{f(z|\mu_{2})}{f(z|\mu_{1})}\mathrm{d}z,\text{with }f(\cdot|\mu)\text{ being pdf of }\mu\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=\{\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})\text{ with }\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\ (d\geq 1):\mu\text{ has a density }f(\cdot|\mu)>0\text{ on }\mathcal{X}.\}.

Appendix B
FDE methods for general state-action space

B.1
Cramer FDE

We construct our objective function (
10
) based on Cramer distance
\mathfrak{d}=l_{2}^{2}
, and refer to this as Cramer FDE. Assuming bounded variables in
\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
Z(s,a;\theta)\in[a,b]
for all
s,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}
and
\theta\in\Theta
, we obtain the following based on Theorem
C.4
, with proof and detailed bound in Appendix
C.8
.

Corollary B.1
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}=l_{2}^{2}
achieves the following in bounded support in
\mathbb{R}
, say
[a,b]
.

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1/p}{2+\alpha}}\bigg\}.

Here,
\lesssim
means upper-bounded by RHS multiplied by some constant that does not depend on
\gamma
and
N
.

Although Cramer FDE is limited in bounded uni-dimensional distributions, we can provide direct comparison with FLE under exactly the same conditions, at least for
d=1
. Assuming bounded return distribution with the same model complexity
\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty},\epsilon)\lesssim\epsilon^{-\alpha}
(where
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Theta}
represents the conditional pdf family modeled by
\Theta
),
Cramer FDE achieves faster convergence rate than FLE uniformly for all
p\geq 1
and
\alpha\in(0,2)
. See Appendix
C.17.1
for its proof.

\displaystyle\text{FLE : }\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\tilde{O}_{P}\big(N^{\frac{-1}{2p}(1-\alpha)}\big)\quad\text{VS}\quad\text{Cramer : }\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\tilde{O}_{P}\big(N^{\frac{-1}{p(2+\alpha)}}\big).

(13)

Here,
\tilde{O}_{P}
indicating the convergence rate, allowing up to logarithmic difference from the conventional
O_{P}
. We have suggested two examples for Cramer FDE, in which FLE cannot provide a valid bound due to no conditional densities. The first example is linear MDP (Appendix
C.17.2
) that is frequently assumed in both traditional and distributional RL
[
60
,
44
,
59
, e.g.,]
. Cramer FDE achieves
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}
-convergence rate of
\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{-1/(3p)})
. The second example is Linear Quadratic Regulator (Appendix
C.17.3
), which has been also been frequently mentioned in traditional RL
[
8
,
35
, e.g.,]
, and was recently applied in distributional RL under finite-horizontal setting
[
61
]
. We extended this towards the infinite-horizontal setting. For bounded distributional families, Cramer FDE achieves
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}
-convergence rate of
\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{-1/(2p)})
. For unbounded distributional families, its generalized extension Energy FDE (introduced in the following subsection) achieves
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}
-convergence rate of
\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{-1/8})
.

B.2
Energy FDE

Now, we will assume multi-dimensional return (
d\geq 1
), but still bounded. Here, we use
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}
with
\beta\in(1,2)
, which we name as Energy FDE.
{\rm MMD}_{\beta}
is the MMD (
18
) with the following

\displaystyle k_{\beta}(x,y):=\|x\|^{\beta}+\|y\|^{\beta}-\|x-y\|^{\beta}\quad(1<\beta<2).

Unlike the tabular setting (supported by Theorem
3.3
) where we could apply
\beta\in(0,1)
, we have to limit to
\beta\in(0,1)
. But we can extend the result into
\beta=1
(second statement of Corollary
B.2
). Considering that
l_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}{\rm MMD}_{\beta=1}^{2}
for
d=1

[
6
]
, Energy FDE can be viewed as an extension of Cramer FDE. Proofs and detailed bounds are in Appendix
C.9
.

Corollary B.2
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}
with
\beta\geq 1
can achieve the following bound in bounded support of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm MMD}}_{\beta,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{\frac{\beta-1}{2}}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1}{2+\alpha}}\bigg\}\quad(1<\beta<2),

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm MMD}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\bigg(\frac{1}{\log(1/\gamma)}\bigg)^{1+\frac{1}{2+\alpha}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{(\log N)^{1+\frac{2}{2+\alpha}}\cdot N^{\frac{-1}{2+\alpha}}\bigg\}\quad(\beta=1).

This result can further be used to bound
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}
-inaccuracy based on the relationship between
{\rm MMD}_{\beta}
and
\mathbb{W}_{1}
based on (
31
), which is introduced by
[
34
]
. Based on
\mathbb{W}_{p}(\mu,\nu)\leq D^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1}^{1/p}(\mu,\nu)
(suggested by
[
42
]
) where
D\in(0,\infty)
represents the diameter of support of bounded distributions, we can further bound
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq D^{2(1-\frac{1}{p})}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}^{1/p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})
. However, these are still restricted in bounded distributions.

Therefore, let us introduce the case where Energy FDE can bound the inaccuracy for unbounded distributions even without
s,a
-conditional densities, based on
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}
-inaccuracy. Towards that end, we assume that the
r
-th moment
(r>1)
of the distributions are uniformly bounded, i.e.,
M_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}(\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta)\|^{r})^{1/r}<\infty
. See Appendix
C.13.3
for proof, which is based on the alternative Theorem
C.11
.

Corollary B.3
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\beta}^{2}
(
\beta=1
), we have following. Assuming that
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta)\|<\infty
and
\sup_{s,a}\|R(s,a)\|_{\psi_{2}}<\infty
where
R(s,a)
indicates the reward vector conditioned on
s,a
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1/2}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{1}{\log(1/\gamma)}\cdot\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{N}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2(l-1)+\alpha}}\bigg\}\quad\text{with }l=\frac{r(2d+3)-1}{r-1}.

Here, having finite values of higher moments (i.e., larger
r>1
) leads to tighter convergence rate. Bounded variables (
r=\infty
) will give us
l=2d+3
, leading to
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})=\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{\frac{-1}{4(d+1)+\alpha}})
. In case of
d=1
and
r=\infty
, it does not degenerate into that of Corollary
B.1
, since they are based upon different proof structures.

B.3
PDF-L2 FDE

Now let us construct the objective function (
10
) with
\mathfrak{d}=d_{L_{2}}^{2}
, namely PDF-L2 method. By assuming existence of conditional densities, PDF-L2 method can achieve faster convergence rate for unbounded distributions than Energy FDE (Corollary
B.3
). Proof and detailed bound are in Appendix
C.10
.

Corollary B.4
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}=d_{L_{2}}^{2}
can achieve the following bound in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assuming
M_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}(\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta)\|^{r})^{1/r}<\infty
with
r>p
and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{z,s,a}|f_{\theta}(z|s,a)|<\infty
.

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1}{2+\alpha}\cdot\frac{2(r-p)}{(d+2r)p}}\bigg\}

We can see that PDF-L2 method not only provides
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}
-bound for all
p\geq 1
, but also provides faster convergence rate than Energy FDE (Corollary
B.3
) in the case of
p=1
, for all
r\geq 1
,
d\geq 1
, and
\alpha\in(0,2)
.

B.4
MMD-Matern FDE

[
54
]
has shown that MMD based on translation invariant kernels, i.e.,
k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\kappa_{0}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})
, can bound Wasserstein metrics when the distributions have smooth enough densities (see their Theorem 15). One such example is MMD-Matern, which is the MMD (
18
) with following kernel with parameters
\nu>0
and
{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}>0
(assumed to be fixed) and
K_{\nu}
being modified Bessel function,

\displaystyle k_{\nu}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\frac{2^{1-\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu)}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\nu}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|}{{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}}\bigg)^{\nu}\cdot K_{\nu}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\nu}\cdot\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|}{{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}}\bigg).

We will treat
{\sigma_{\rm Mat}}>0
as a fixed constant since we are more interested in
\nu
. It is well-known that
\nu=1
corresponds to Laplace kernel and
\nu\rightarrow\infty
corresponds to RBF kernel. It leads to following result (proof and detailed bound in Appendix
C.11
).

Corollary B.5
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\nu}^{2}
can achieve the following bound in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assuming
M_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}(\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta)\|^{r})^{1/r}<\infty
with
r>p
and its conditional densities having Sobolev norms
\|f_{\theta}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{H^{\nu+d/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq B(<\infty)
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1}{2+\alpha}\cdot\frac{r-p}{(d+2r)p}}\bigg\}.

Although this can be applied for unbounded distributions as PDF-L2 method (Corollary
B.4
), its bound is always looser than that of PDF-L2 method, as shown in its convergence rate which is half times that of PDF-L2 method. This is since the proof of bounding Wasserstein metric by MMD-Matern is based on the following logic.

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\lesssim d_{L_{2}}^{\frac{2(r-p)}{(d+2r)p}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\lesssim{\rm MMD}_{\nu}^{\frac{r-p}{(d+2r)p}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).

See Section 2.4 of
[
54
]
for details. This applies for all possible MMD’s associated with translation-invariant kernels, other than MMD-Matern.

B.5
MMD-RBF FDE

Since MMD-Matern that is shown above covers the case of
0<\nu<\infty
and requires smooth enough densities. MMD-RBF (corresponding to MMD-Matern with
\nu=\infty
) is used a lot in many machine learning areas (e.g., kernel support vector machine).

\displaystyle k_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{(2\sqrt{\pi})^{d}}\cdot{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{-d}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-1}{4{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}\bigg)\quad\text{with }{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}>0.

It is also used in distributional reinforcement learning (MMDRL by
[
38
]
). They only used it in simulations, without any theoretical justification due to their failure to provide a contractive metric based on MMD-RBF. Following lemma can provide justification of its practice (proof and detailed bound in Appendix
C.12.2
), not requiring the existence of conditional densities.

Corollary B.6
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, assuming
M_{r}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}(\mathbb{E}\|Z(s,a;\theta)\|^{r})^{1/r}<\infty
with
r>p
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}
with
{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\sigma_{N}>0
can achieve the following bound in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot C(p,d,\sigma_{N},p)\cdot\bigg(\frac{C_{brack}(\sigma_{N})}{\sigma_{N}^{d}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha/2}\cdot\frac{r-p}{(d+2r)p}}

\displaystyle\times O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1}{2+\alpha}\cdot\frac{2(r-p)}{(d+2r)p}}\bigg\}+\underbrace{\frac{2^{\frac{5}{2}-\frac{1}{2p}}}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma((p+d)/2)}{\Gamma(p/2)}\cdot\sigma_{N}}_{\text{non-random quantity decreasing by }\sigma_{N}\rightarrow 0.}.

Fixing
\sigma_{N}={\sigma_{\rm RBF}}>0
leads to an irreducible term at the end. Of course, we can shrink it by letting
\sigma_{N}\rightarrow 0
. However, other terms (e.g.,
C(p,d,\sigma_{N},p)
,
C_{brack}(\sigma_{N})/\sigma_{N}^{d}
) can increase with
\sigma_{N}\rightarrow 0
, leaving the optimal rate of
\sigma_{N}
to be intractable.

Although we cannot show that MMD-RBF can shrink Wasserstein inaccuracy to zero, it can bound gaussian-smoothed Wasserstein metric
\mathbb{W}_{p}^{\sigma}(\mu,\nu):=\mathbb{W}_{p}(\mu*\alpha_{\sigma},\nu*\alpha_{\sigma})
where
*
indicates convolution and
\alpha_{\sigma}
is the probability measure of
N(\mathbf{0},\sigma^{2}I_{d})
. This is widely used as an alternative for Wasserstein metric that is difficult to be computed in high dimensions
d>1

[
68
,
39
, e.g.,]
. Thus, we have made a separate lemma (Lemma
C.8
) that can accommodate many other probability distances that can bound smoothed gaussain Wasserstein metric, which includes MMD-Two-Moment.

B.6
MMD-RBF-transformed FDE

[
68
]
suggested an MMD (
18
) with the following kernel which is RBF kernel multiplied with an extra term. We will refer to its corresponding
{\rm MMD}
as MMD-RBF-transformed, denoting it as
{\rm MMD}_{(\sigma,f)}
.

\displaystyle k_{(\sigma,f)}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\exp\bigg(-\frac{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}}\bigg)\cdot I_{f}\bigg(\frac{\|\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}\|}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\bigg).

The conditions of density function
f
and definition of
I_{f}
are mentioned in Equations 8 and 10 of
[
68
]
. Assuming bounded kernel
k_{(\sigma,f)}(\cdot,\cdot)<\infty
for fixed value of
\sigma>0
, we can achieve the following. Proof and detailed bound are in Appendix
C.12.3
.

Corollary B.7
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}=d_{L_{2}}^{2}
can achieve the following bound in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if we have
k_{(\sigma,f)}<\infty
for fixed value of
\sigma>0
.

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{\sigma_{N}}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot\bigg(C_{max}^{(\sigma_{N},f)}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}(\sigma_{N})\bigg)^{\frac{1/p}{1+\alpha/2}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1/p}{2+\alpha}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot 2^{\frac{5}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\cdot\frac{\Gamma((p+d)/2)}{\Gamma(p/2)}\cdot\sigma_{N}

We can let
\sigma_{N}\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. However, the optimal rate is intractable.

One example of such
f
is generalized beta-prime distributions:
f(x)=\frac{\epsilon}{2\pi x}\cdot((\frac{x}{\lambda})^{-\epsilon}+(\frac{x}{\lambda})^{\epsilon})^{-1}
with
\epsilon\in(0,d+2p]
and
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
, which leads to Two-moment kernel (see Definition 1 of
[
68
]
). They have shown that such
k_{(\sigma,f)}
is bounded in their Section 3.2 for bounded distributions.

B.7
MMD-Coulomb FDE

MMD-Coulumb is an MMD which corresponds to the following kernel.

\displaystyle k_{cou}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\kappa_{0}^{(cou)}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}):=\begin{cases}-\log\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|&\text{if}\quad d=2\\
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{2-d}&\text{if}\quad d\geq 3\end{cases}

We can see that
k(\cdot,\cdot)<\infty
does not hold, leading to violation of the first statement of (
20
). Since we cannot apply Theorem
C.4
, we should resort to Theorem
C.11
instead, which gives us the following result for MMD-Coulomb. Proof and detailed bound are in Appendix
C.13.2
.

Corollary B.8
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) based on
\mathfrak{d}={\rm MMD}_{cou}^{2}
can achieve the following bound. Assuming bounded support in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 2
) and
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}\mathcal{E}_{cou}(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a))<\infty
with
\mathcal{E}_{cou}(\mu):=k_{cou}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mu(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x})\mu(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y})
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\frac{1}{\log(1/\gamma)}\cdot\bigg(\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{N}\bigg)^{\frac{6-\alpha}{16p}}\bigg\}

We can further bound
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq D^{2(1-\frac{1}{p})}\cdot\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}^{1/p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})
if we assume bounded conditional distributions. Note that the condition
\mathcal{E}(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a))<\infty
does not allow nonzero probability to any points.

B.8
KL FDE

Interestingly enough, convergence of FLE
[
61
]
can also be shown by Theorem
D.1
. However, since it is based on maximium likelihood, which is not a squared metric, we could fit this into the structure of neither Theorem
C.4
nor
C.11
. Instead, we developed a method that uses log-likelihood (or equivalently, KL divergence), based on Theorem
D.1
. See Appendix
C.14
for its proof. Note that this is different from FLE in the sense that it fully utilizes the closed form density of
\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
and
\pi(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})
needed for
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
in computing the objective function (
10
). This is free from MC error caused by sampling from
z\sim\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
as
[
61
]
did.

Corollary B.9
.

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, our estimator (
10
) with
\mathfrak{d}={\rm KL}
(Kullback-Leibler divergence) achieves the following bound. Assuming bounded distributions and existence of conditional densities, we have following with
T=\lfloor\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{2p}}\cdot\frac{1/p}{2+\alpha}\cdot\log_{1/\gamma}N\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\lesssim\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2p}}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-1/p}{2+\alpha}}\bigg\}.

Here, we have
\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Theta}^{1/2},\|\cdot\|_{L_{2},\rho},\epsilon)\leq C_{brack}\cdot\epsilon^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in(0,2)
with
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Theta}^{1/2}
being the squared conditional densities, i.e.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Theta}^{1/2}:=\{f_{\theta}^{1/2}(\cdot|\cdots):\theta\in\Theta\}
.

Appendix C
Technical proofs

C.1
Functional Bregman divergence

C.1.1
Technical definition

Bregman divergence was originally developed to measure discrepancy between two real vectors. It measures the difference between the value of the convex function
\phi
at
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and its first-order approximation at
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle D_{\phi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\phi(\mathbf{x})-\phi(\mathbf{y})-\langle\nabla\phi(\mathbf{y}),\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\rangle.

After defining a scalar-valued convex function
\Phi
over a functional inner product space, they can also be extended to functional objects based on Frechet derivative
[
18
]
.

\displaystyle D_{\Phi}(f,g)=\Phi(f)-\Phi(g)-\langle\delta\Phi(g),f-g\rangle.

Based on the fact that each probability measure can have its own functional representation (e.g., density functions), we can also extend functional Bregman divergence into discrepancy between two probability measures. Following is the technical definition of a functional Bregman divergence for two probability measures
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}
(Section 3.2 of
[
41
]
).

Definition C.1
.

Linear span of
\mathcal{P}
, namely
\text{span}\mathcal{P}
, is a collection of signed measures, and let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\text{span}\mathcal{P}
be a convex subset that contains
\mathcal{P}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. Let
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})
be the functional space where its arbitrary element
f
satisfies
\int_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}\big|f\big|\mathrm{d}\mu<\infty
for
\forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}
with
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}
being the support space. Now, assume a strictly convex function
\Phi:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that has a subgradient
\Phi^{*}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})
which satisfies
\Phi(Q)\geq\Phi^{*}(P)\cdot(Q-P)+\Phi(P)
for all
P,Q\in\mathcal{U}
, where
f\cdot\mu:=\int_{\Omega_{\mathcal{X}}}f\mathrm{d}\mu
for any
f\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{P})
and
\mu\in\text{span}\mathcal{P}
. Then we can build a functional Bregman divergence
\mathfrak{d}:\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
on
\mathcal{U}
as follows (Definition 3.8 of
[
41
]
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(P,Q)=\Phi(Q)-\Phi^{*}(P)\cdot(Q-P)-\Phi(P).

Since functional Bregman divergence in Definition
C.1
is based on a strictly convex function
\Phi
, equality
\mathfrak{d}(P,Q)=0
holds only when
P=Q
. This is needed to ensure that
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon
becomes the unique minimizer of (
9
). Note that this is stronger than general definition of functional Bregman divergence (e.g., Definition 3.8 of
[
41
]
) that is based on standard convex function (which may not be strictly convex).

C.1.2
Proof of Theorem
2.4

We will treat both
S,A\sim\rho
and
R,S^{\prime}\sim{\tilde{p}}(\cdots|S,A)
to be random. Let
\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
be arbitrary.
We obtain the following, by using the same logic that
[
2
]
used in their Theorem 1,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi\big(\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})\big)-\Phi\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A)\big)-\Phi^{*}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A)\big)\cdot\big(\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})-\Upsilon_{1}(S,A)\big)\bigg\}.

Letting
\Upsilon_{2}=\Upsilon
and
\Upsilon_{1}=\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi^{*}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(S,A)\big)\cdot\big(\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon)-\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(S,A)\big)\bigg\}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi^{*}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(S,A)\big)\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime};\Upsilon)-\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(S,A)\ \bigg|\ S,A\bigg\}\bigg\}=0,

\displaystyle\therefore\ \mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon)\big)\bigg\}=\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi\big(\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon)\big)\bigg\}-\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(S,A)\big)\bigg\}.

Then, the following holds based on
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)=\mathbb{E}\{\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})|s,a\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})\big)\bigg\}-\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2}(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2}(S,A)\big)\bigg\}-\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A)\big)\bigg\}-\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\Phi^{*}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A)\big)\cdot\big(\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})-\Upsilon_{1}(S,A)\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2}(S,A)\big)\bigg\}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})\big)\bigg\}=\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{\rho}(\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2}(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{2})\big)\bigg\}.

(14)

where
\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{\rho}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{\mathfrak{d}(\Upsilon_{1}(S,A),\Upsilon_{2}(S,A))\}
. Note that we definition of our empirical and population objective functions (
5
) and (
9
) already require
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime};\Upsilon)\in\mathcal{P}
. Otherwise, they will not be defined. Then, by convexity of
\mathcal{P}
, we have
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}
, since it is a convex combination over
r,s^{\prime}\sim p(\cdot|s,a)
(see definition (
3
)). This leads to satisfaction of (
9
), since
\mathcal{P}
is a convex set.

To ensure
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime};\Upsilon)\in\mathcal{P}
, we can assume that
\mathcal{P}
is closed under push-forward transformation with respect to single-sample Bellman backup functions
\{g_{r,\gamma}\}
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that can be observed. For a single-sampe Bellman backup function defined as
g_{r,\gamma}(x):=r+\gamma x
and
X\sim\mu
, its push-forward measure
(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\mu
is defined as the distribution of
r+\gamma X
. Being closed under push-forward transformation means that
(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\mu\in\mathcal{P}
holds for any probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{P}
and any
r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that can be observed. Then, for an arbitrary
\Upsilon\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
, we can ensure that
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon)\in\mathcal{P}
holds for any target policy
\pi:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})
and observable
(r,s^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}
, by its definition (
4
) and convexity of
\mathcal{P}
. Then, our objective functions (
5
) and (
9
) can always be defined.

C.2
How bounding iteration-level error leads to final inaccuracy

By Definition
2.2
, we can prove (
6
) based on
\Upsilon_{\pi}=\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\pi}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}(\Upsilon_{T},\Upsilon_{\pi})

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\eta}(\Upsilon_{T},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{T-1})+\tilde{\eta}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{T-1},\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq\tilde{\eta}(\Upsilon_{T},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{T-1})+\zeta\cdot\tilde{\eta}(\Upsilon_{T-1},\Upsilon_{\pi})

\displaystyle\leq\cdots\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot\tilde{\eta}(\Upsilon_{t},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{t-1})+\zeta^{T}\cdot\tilde{\eta}(\Upsilon_{0},\Upsilon_{\pi}).

This leads to (
6
).

C.3
How extended metrics become contractive

C.3.1
Expectation-extension

Let us show that the expectation-extended (
8
) metric satisfies Definition
2.2
.

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}^{2q}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})=\mathbb{E}_{s,a\sim d_{\pi}}\bigg[\bigg\{\underbrace{\eta^{q}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\big)}_{\text{(A)}}\bigg\}^{2}\bigg]

Here, the (A) part can be bounded as follows based on Properties (
16
). Letting
{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},a^{\prime}|s,a)={\tilde{p}}(r,s^{\prime}|s,a)\cdot\pi(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})
and temporarily using it as a probability measure, we have

(A)

\displaystyle=\eta^{q}\bigg\{\int(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\Upsilon_{1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},a^{\prime}|s,a),\int(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\Upsilon_{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},a^{\prime}|s,a)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\int\eta^{q}\bigg\{(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\Upsilon_{1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),(g_{r,\gamma})_{\#}\Upsilon_{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\bigg\}\mathrm{d}{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},a^{\prime}|s,a)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{cq}\cdot\int\eta^{q}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),\Upsilon_{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\big)\mathrm{d}{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},a^{\prime}|s,a),

(15)

where we have used the (C) property of (
16
) in the second line, and the (S) and (L) properties of (
16
) in the last line. Then, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}^{2q}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2cq}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{s,a\sim d_{\pi}}\bigg[\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\sim{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(\cdots|s,a)}\big(\eta^{q}(\Upsilon_{1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),\Upsilon_{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}))\big)\bigg\}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2cq}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{s,a\sim d_{\pi}}\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime},a^{\prime}\sim{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(\cdots|s,a)}\bigg\{\eta^{2q}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime}),\Upsilon_{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2cq-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{s,a\sim d_{\pi}}\bigg\{\eta^{2q}\big(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\big)\bigg\},

We acknowledge that the last line can be shown by the trick used by
[
61
]
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{s},\tilde{a}\sim d_{\pi}}{\tilde{p}}^{\pi}(s,a|\tilde{s},\tilde{a})\leq\gamma^{-1}\cdot d_{\pi}(s,a)
. This finally leads to

\displaystyle\therefore\ \bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2}).

C.3.2
Supremum-extension

We can apply the same logic to supremum-extension (
7
). Taking up from (
15
), we have

\displaystyle(A)\leq\gamma^{cq}\cdot\sup_{s,a}\eta\big(\Upsilon_{1}(s,a),\Upsilon_{2}(s,a)\big).

Taking supremum over
\mathcal{S\times A}
on the LHS gives us
\eta_{\infty}^{q}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})\leq\gamma^{cq}\cdot\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})
, leading to
\eta_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{1},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{2})\leq\gamma^{c}\cdot\eta_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{1},\Upsilon_{2})
. Although Theorem 4.25 of
[
5
]
presented the same statement, their proof requires an additional assumption, i.e.,
R
and
S^{\prime}
(generated from
{\tilde{p}}(\cdots|s,a)
) being independent. We did not require this assumption.

C.4
Examples of probability metric

To construct a metric
\tilde{\eta}
over
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
that satisfies Definition
2.2
, we will assume to have a probability metric
\eta
over
\mathcal{P}
that satisfies the following three properties of (
16
) with some
c>0
and
q\geq 1
. We copied the terminologies from
[
5
]
(see their Definitions 4.22, 4.23, 4.24). In the first two properties,
\mathcal{L}(X)
denotes the probability measure of random vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
,
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})
for some space
\mathcal{X}
can be arbitrary in the third property.

\displaystyle\text{{(S) } $c$-scale-sensitive :}\ \eta(\mathcal{L}(\gamma X),\mathcal{L}(\gamma Y))\leq\gamma^{c}\cdot\eta(\mathcal{L}(X),\mathcal{L}(Y))

(16)

\displaystyle\text{{(L) } location-insensitive :}\ \eta(\mathcal{L}(X+z),\mathcal{L}(Y+z))\leq\eta(\mathcal{L}(X),\mathcal{L}(Y))\ \text{ for an arbitrary }z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\text{{(C) } $q$-convex :}\ \eta^{q}\bigg\{\int\mu_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(x),\int\mu_{2}(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\bigg\}\leq\int\eta^{q}\bigg\{\mu_{1}(x),\mu_{2}(x)\bigg\}\mathrm{d}\nu(x).

Note that if some
q\geq 1
satisfies the Property-3, then any
q^{\prime}\geq q
also satisfies it by Jensen’s inequality.

Here are the examples that satisfy the properties in (
16
). We specify the values of
c,q>0
for each
\eta
. Then, we specify the contractive factor
\zeta=\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}
for their corresponding expectation-extended metrics (
8
). (Note that the supremum-extension (
7
) has
\zeta=\gamma^{c}
.)

\displaystyle\text{Wasserstein-p metric }(\mathbb{W}_{p})

\displaystyle:\quad c=1,\quad q\geq p\quad\Rightarrow\quad\zeta=\gamma^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}

(17)

\displaystyle\text{MMD-Energy }({\rm MMD}_{\beta})

\displaystyle:\quad c=\frac{\beta}{2},\quad q\geq 1\text{ and }q>\frac{1}{\beta}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\zeta=\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}(\beta-\frac{1}{q})}

\displaystyle\text{CDF }L_{p}-\text{metric }(l_{p})

\displaystyle:\quad c=\frac{1}{p},\quad q\geq p\quad\Rightarrow\quad\zeta=\gamma^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2q}}

Here are the definition of above metrics.
\mathbb{W}_{p}
is the Wasserstein metric, which is defined as follows with
J(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
being the possible joint distributions of
\mathbf{X}\sim\mu_{1}
and
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mu_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\inf_{J(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\|\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{Y}\|^{p}\bigg)^{1/p}.

It is straightforward to see Properties 1 and 2 hold with
c=1
. Property 3 is shown to hold by
[
25
]
(see their Appendix A.3.1).

MMD (maximum-mean discepancy) defined as follows
[
24
]

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{k}^{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\mathbb{E}\{k(X,X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k(Y,Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k(X,Y)\},

(18)

with
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\sim\mu_{1}
and
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\sim\mu_{2}
are all independent.
{\rm MMD}_{\beta}
is associated with Energy kernel
k_{\beta}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\|\mathbf{x}\|^{\beta}+\|\mathbf{y}\|^{\beta}-\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|^{\beta}
(
0<\beta<2
),
Properties 1 and 2 are straightforward t show with
c=\beta/2
. Property 3 can be shown with
q=1
, since MMD can be expressed as the norm of difference between two mean embeddings, i.e.,
{\rm MMD}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\|\kappa_{\mu_{1}}-\kappa_{\mu_{2}}\|_{\mathcal{H}})
where
\kappa_{\mu}
is the mean embedding of
\mu
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}}
is the corresponding RKHS norm of RKHS
\mathcal{H}
.

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}\bigg(\int\mu_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(x),\int\mu_{2}(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\bigg)=\bigg\|\int\kappa_{\mu_{1}(x)}\mathrm{d}\nu(x)-\kappa_{\mu_{2}(x)}\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\bigg\|_{\mathcal{H}}=

\displaystyle\bigg\|\int\big\{\kappa_{\mu_{1}(x)}-\kappa_{\mu_{2}(x)}\big\}\mathrm{d}\nu(x)\bigg\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\int\|\kappa_{\mu_{1}(x)}-\kappa_{\mu_{2}(x)}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{d}\nu(x)=\int{\rm MMD}(\mu_{1}(x),\mu_{2}(x))\mathrm{d}\nu(x).

l_{p}
is the metric between cdf’s of 1-dimensional random variable. Letting
F_{i}
be the cdf of
\mu_{i}
, we define

\displaystyle l_{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\bigg(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)|^{p}\mathrm{d}z\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Properties 1 and 2 are shown with
c=1/p
by Proposition 3.2 of
[
40
]
. Property 3 can be shown by using the fact that
l_{p}
is a functional
L_{p}
norm between cdf’s. The same logic holds for
d_{L_{p}}
.

Table
3
also contains the survey of other well-known probability metrics. We will skip proof for their satisfaction or non-satisfaction of each property. With
f_{i}
and
F_{i}
being the pdf and cdf of probability measure
\mu_{i}
,

Hellinger metric:

\displaystyle h(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\sqrt{f_{1}(z)}-\sqrt{f_{2}(z)}\bigg)^{2}\mathrm{d}z\bigg\}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\text{PDF }L_{p}\text{-metric: }

\displaystyle d_{L_{p}}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg|f_{1}(z)-f_{2}(z)\bigg|^{p}\mathrm{d}z\bigg)^{1/p},

Discrepancy metric:

\displaystyle d_{D}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\sup_{\text{closed balls }B}\big|\mu_{1}(B)-\mu_{2}(B)\big|,

Kolmogorov metric:

\displaystyle d_{K}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|F_{1}(x)-F_{2}(x)\big|.

Table 3:
Survey of metrics: If it satisfies
c
-scale-sensitive or
q
-convexity, the corresponding value of
c>0
or
q\geq 1
are specified.

(\mathbb{W}_{p})

with
0<\beta<2
)

{\rm MMD}_{\nu}
with
0<\nu\leq\infty
)

(

)

(

) :
p=1
refers to TVD

In Table
3
, MMD’s associated with any kernel satisfy convexity with
q=1
. However, MMD-Energy was so far the only example that we have found, which satisfies all three properties. In the definitions of Wasserstein metric and MMD-Energy (Appendix
C.4
), the Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
used can be replaced with another norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. See Example 11 of
[
22
]
and Proposition 3 of
[
47
]
for extensions of MMD-Energy, and
[

]
for extensions of Wasserstein metrics.

C.5
Functional spaces and corresponding norms

Following are examples of the functional inner product space
(\mathcal{G},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m})
and its corresponding probability space
\mathcal{P}
that we have used for our methods in Section
3.1
.

For
m=l_{2}
(only applying for
d=1
), the functional representer is the cdf, denoted by
F(\cdot|\mu)
. The functional space is the functional
L_{2}
space, that is
\mathcal{G}:=\{F:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\int|F(z)|^{2}\mathrm{d}z<\infty\}
with
\mathcal{X}=[b_{1},b_{2}]\subsetneq\mathbb{R}
being a bounded support. The equipped inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L_{2}}
is defined as
\langle F_{1},F_{2}\rangle_{L_{2}}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}F_{1}(z)F_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}z
. The functional representer of
\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}
, namely
F_{\theta}(\cdot|s,a)
will be an element of the following set
\mathcal{F}
:

\displaystyle m=l_{2}\ (d=1)\

\displaystyle:\ \ \mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\big\{F:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]\ \big|\ F\text{ is a cdf.}\big\}\subsetneq\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle:\ \ \mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\Delta(\mathcal{X}):=\big\{\text{probability measures that have support in }\mathcal{X}\big\}.

For
m=d_{L_{2}}
, the functional representer is the pdf, denoted by
f(\cdot|\mu)
. The functional space is the functional
L_{2}
space, that is
\mathcal{G}:=\{f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\int|f(z)|^{2}\mathrm{d}z<\infty\}
with
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
being the support. The equipped inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L_{2}}
is defined as
\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{L_{2}}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f_{1}(z)f_{2}(z)\mathrm{d}z
. The functional representer of
\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}
, namely
f_{\theta}(\cdot|s,a)
will be an element of the following set
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
:

\displaystyle m=d_{L_{2}}\

\displaystyle:\ \ \widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\big\{f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\ \big|\ f\text{ is a pdf and }\|f(\cdot|\mu)\|_{L_{2}}<\infty\big\}\subsetneq\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle:\ \ \mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}\ \big|\ \mu\text{ has a pdf and }\|f(\cdot|\mu)\|_{L_{2}}<\infty\}.

For
m={\rm MMD}_{k}
(maximum mean discrepancy associated with some kernel), the functional representer is the kernel mean embedding,
\kappa(\cdot|\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mu}\{k(Z,\cdot)\}
. The functional space is the RKHS corresponding to the given kernel, i.e.,
\mathcal{G}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\cdot k(x_{i},\cdot)|\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R},x_{i}\in\mathcal{X},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
with
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
being the support. The equipped inner product is the corresponding RKHS inner product,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
that is defined as
\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}k(x_{i},\cdot),\sum_{j=1}^{m}\beta_{j}k(x_{j}^{\prime},\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}k(x_{i},x_{j}^{\prime})
. The functional representer of
\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}
, namely
\kappa_{\theta}(\cdot|s,a)
will be an element of the following set
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle m={\rm MMD}\

\displaystyle:\ \ \mathcal{H}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\{\kappa:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ |\ \kappa(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mu}\{k(Z,\cdot)\}\ \text{for some }\mu\in\mathcal{P}\}\subsetneq\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle:\ \ \mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}\big\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}\ \big|\ \|\kappa(\cdot|\mu)\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\infty\big\}.

C.6
Proof of Theorem
C.4

For our analysis, we define the population objective function and the target parameter at each iteration:

\displaystyle F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{Z}_{t}}\mathbb{E}_{R,S^{\prime}\sim{\tilde{p}}(\cdot|s,a)}\bigg\{\mathfrak{d}\Big(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\Big)\bigg\}.

(19)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{t}:=\{(s,a)\ |\ (s,a,r,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}\}
. Note that we have
{\theta_{*,t}}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
. Like Section
3.1
, our objective functions should have connection with the surrogate metric
m
, although
\mathfrak{d}
may not necessarily be the squared form of
m
(i.e.,
\mathfrak{d}\neq m^{2}
).
With
m
being a probability metric for
\mathcal{P}
and extended metrics
\bar{m}_{\rho,1},\bar{m}_{n,1},m_{\infty}
for
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
(defined in (
21
)),
they should satisfy following Assumption
C.2

with
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}(n)}
being
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
-conditioned subgaussian norm (defined in (
27
) of Appendix
C.8
).

Assumption C.2

(Norm-space association)
.

The functional Bregman divergence
\mathfrak{d}
and probability metric
m
over
\mathcal{P}
form a norm-space association (NS association, in short) if there exists an norm space
(\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{m})
where
any element
\mu\in\mathcal{P}
can be uniquely (up to equivalence under zero norm
\|\cdot\|_{m}
) represented by the corresponding element
G(\cdot|\mu)\in\mathcal{G}
such that

(NS1)

The
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
-conditioned modulus
\Delta_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}):=\{{\hat{F}_{n,t}}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\}-\{{\hat{F}_{n,t}}({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\}
is a mean-zero sub-gaussian process with respect to
\bar{m}_{n,1}
. That is, for any
\mathcal{Z}_{t}\subset\mathcal{S\times A}
and
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}\in\Theta
, we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{n}({\theta_{1}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-\Delta_{n}({\theta_{2}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\|_{{\psi_{2}(n)}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}).

(NS2)

We have
F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\geq\lambda\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}^{l}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})
for some
l\geq 2
. Here, the value of
\lambda>0
does not depend on
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
,
\theta
,
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
(or
{\theta_{*,t}}
such that
\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}=\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
).

(NS3)

\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\rightarrow^{P}0
converges in a rate that does not depend on
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
(and thereby
{\theta_{*,t}}
) and the deterministic sequence
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
.

(NS4)

Elements of
\mathcal{M}_{\Theta}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
has a corresponding element in
\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
where
\mathcal{G}
is a functional norm space with
\|\cdot\|_{m}
, such that
\|G_{\mu_{1}}-G_{\mu_{2}}\|_{m}:=m(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. With the extended norm
\|\cdot\|_{m,\infty}
corresponding to
m_{\infty}
(see (
21
)) and some
\alpha\in(0,2)
, it satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{z,s,a}|G_{\theta}(z|s,a)|<\infty\ \&\ \log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{G}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{m,\infty},\epsilon)\leq C_{brack}\cdot\epsilon^{-\alpha}.

(20)

It is possible that there exists an element in
\mathcal{G}
that does not represent any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}
.

Under coverage assumption (Assumption
C.3
), we can obtain Theorem
C.4
, with its proof in the subsections of Appendix
C.6
.

Assumption C.3
.

(Coverage)
C_{cover}:=\sup_{\theta,{\tilde{\theta}}\in\Theta}\bar{m}_{d_{\pi},1}^{2}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\tilde{\theta}}})/\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{2}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})<\infty
.

Theorem C.4
.

Suppose Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
,
C.3
hold. Moreover, given a probability metric
\eta
that satisfies (S-L-C) with convexity parameter
q\geq 1
and scale-sensitivity parameter
c>1/(2q)
(i.e., (
16
) of Appendix
C.4
),
consider the FDE with a functional Bregman divergence
\mathfrak{d}
satisfying Assumption
C.2
of Appendix
C.6
.
Then, by letting
T=\lfloor\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}\cdot\log_{1/\gamma}N\rfloor
with
\delta^{\prime}=\min\{\delta,1/q\}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}}\cdot C_{0}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}}\bigg\}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}}\cdot 2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0},

where
C_{0}>0
is a constant that contains the effect of the parameters
C_{surr}
,
C_{cover}
,
C_{brack}
,
C_{subg}
, etc (defined in (
24
) of Appendix
C.6
).
Note that we need
q>1/(2c)
to ensure
\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\in(0,1)
.

C.6.1
Convergence in a single step

Lemma C.5
.

Assume that our objective functions (
10
) and (
19
) and
\eta
satisfy Assumption
C.2
.
Conditioning on any given value of
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
(previous iteration value), under Assumptions
3.1
,
C.3
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}})\leq C_{cover}^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(C_{subg}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\alpha/2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l-1+\alpha/2}}\cdot O_{P}\big(n^{\frac{-1}{2(l-1)+\alpha}}\big).

The proof of Lemma
C.5
is as follows. Let
\mathcal{D}_{t}:\{(s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

With the norm representation of the surrogate metric
m
, i.e.,
m(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\|G_{\mu_{1}}(\cdot)-G_{\mu_{2}}(\cdot)\|_{m}
, then we can build its extension as follows:

empirical norm :

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{n,1}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}})=\|G_{\theta_{1}}-G_{\theta_{2}}\|_{m,n}:=\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{\theta_{1}}(\cdot|s_{i},a_{i})-G_{\theta_{2}}(\cdot|s_{i},a_{i})\|_{m}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

expectation norm :

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}})=\|G_{\theta_{1}}-G_{\theta_{2}}\|_{m,\rho}=\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{s,a\sim\rho}\|G_{\theta_{1}}(\cdot|s,a)-G_{\theta_{2}}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{m}^{2}\bigg)^{1/2},

supremum norm :

\displaystyle m_{\infty}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}})=\|G_{\theta_{1}}-G_{\theta_{2}}\|_{m,\infty}:=\sup_{s,a}\|G_{\theta_{1}}(\cdot|s,a)-G_{\theta_{2}}(\cdot|s,a)\|_{m}.

(21)

Define
\Theta_{n}^{\delta}:=\{\theta\in\Theta\ :\ \bar{m}_{n,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq\delta\}
. By (NS1), we can apply Theorem 8.1.3 of
[
55
]
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}^{\delta}}\big|\Delta_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big|\bigg\}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta_{n}^{\delta},\bar{m}_{n,1},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta_{n}^{\delta},\bar{m}_{n,1},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot\int_{0}^{\delta}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,m_{\infty},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon.

Let
C_{bound}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{z,s,a}|G_{\theta}(z|s,a)|\in(0,\infty)
. Here, we let
G_{\theta}^{\prime}(\cdot|\cdots):=\frac{1}{C_{bound}}\cdot G_{\theta}(\cdot|\cdots)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}^{\prime}:=\{G_{\theta}^{\prime}|G_{\theta}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}\}
. Note that we have
G_{\theta}^{\prime}(z|s,a)\leq 1
and

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}^{\prime},\|\cdot\|_{m,\infty},\epsilon)=\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{G}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{m,\infty},C_{bound}\cdot\epsilon)\leq C_{brack}\cdot C_{bound}^{-\alpha}\cdot\epsilon^{-\alpha}.

(22)

Let
\bar{m}_{n,1}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{C_{bound}}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}
,
\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{C_{bound}}\cdot\bar{m}_{\rho,1}
,
m_{\infty}^{\prime}:=\frac{1}{C_{bound}}\cdot m_{\infty}
, and we have

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{n,1}^{\prime}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}):=\|G_{\theta_{1}}^{\prime}-G_{\theta_{2}}^{\prime}\|_{m,n},\quad\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{\prime}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}):=\|G_{\theta_{1}}^{\prime}-G_{\theta_{2}}^{\prime}\|_{m,\rho},\quad m_{\infty}^{\prime}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}):=\|G_{\theta_{1}}^{\prime}-G_{\theta_{2}}^{\prime}\|_{m,\infty}.

This leads to

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,m_{\infty},\epsilon)\leq\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,m_{\infty}^{\prime},\epsilon/C_{bound})\leq\log\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}^{\prime},\|\cdot\|_{m,\infty},\epsilon/C_{bound})

\displaystyle\leq\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}^{\prime}\leq\|\cdot\|_{m,\infty},2\epsilon/C_{bound})\leq C_{brack}\cdot C_{bound}^{-\alpha}\cdot\bigg(\frac{2\epsilon}{C_{bound}}\bigg)^{-\alpha}\leq C_{brack}\cdot 2^{-\alpha}\cdot\epsilon^{-\alpha},

\displaystyle\therefore\ \mathbb{E}\bigg\{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}^{\delta}}\big|\Delta_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big|\bigg\}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\alpha/2}\cdot\delta^{1-\alpha/2}.

We have consistency
\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\rightarrow^{P}0
by (NS3). Then, we can apply Theorem
D.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\leq\bigg(C_{subg}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}}{1-\alpha/2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l-1+\alpha/2}}\cdot O_{P}\big(n^{\frac{-1}{2(l-1)+\alpha}}\big).

Using
\frac{1}{2+\alpha}\geq\frac{1}{2(l-1)+\alpha}
due to
l\geq 2
, we can apply logic of Theorem 6.3.2 of
[
52
]
(or equivalently, Theorem 2.3 of
[
32
]
), based on
G_{\theta}^{\prime}(z|s,a)\leq 1
and (
22
). This gives us the same asymptotic bound for
\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})
, and further applying Assumption
C.3
gives us Lemma
C.5
.

C.6.2
Convergence through multiple steps

Now let us bound
\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})
with
q\geq 1
, with Assumption
3.2
. We use
(a+b)^{2q}\leq 2^{2q-1}\cdot(a^{2q}+b^{2q})
to obtain the following:

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})

\displaystyle=\big(\mathbb{E}\{\eta^{2q}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}(s,a))\}\big)^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[2^{2q-1}\cdot\bigg\{C_{surr}^{2q}\cdot m^{2q\cdot\delta}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}(s,a),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s,a))+\epsilon_{0}^{2q}\bigg\}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2q}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot C_{surr}\cdot\big(\mathbb{E}\{m^{2q\delta}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}(s,a))\}\big)^{\frac{1}{2q}}+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle=2C_{surr}\cdot\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\{m^{2q^{\prime}}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}(s,a)\}^{\frac{1}{2q^{\prime}}}\bigg\}^{\delta}+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}\quad\text{where }q^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}q\delta.

Assume a bounded random variable
X
such that
|X|\leq C_{maxim}
. Since
|X/C_{maxim}|\leq 1
, we have the following if
1\leq p_{1}\leq p_{2}
.

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathbb{E}\bigg|\frac{X}{C_{maxim}}\bigg|^{p_{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\leq\bigg(\mathbb{E}\bigg|\frac{X}{C_{maxim}}\bigg|^{p_{1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}=\bigg\{\bigg(\mathbb{E}\bigg|\frac{X}{C_{maxim}}\bigg|^{p_{1}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}\bigg\}^{\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}}},

\displaystyle\therefore\ \big(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p_{2}}\big)^{1/p_{2}}\leq C_{maxim}^{1-\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}}}\cdot\big\{(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p_{1}})^{1/p_{1}}\big\}^{p_{1}/p_{2}}.

If we have
1<q^{\prime}
, letting
p_{1}=2,p_{2}=2q^{\prime}
gives us

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{surr}\cdot\bigg\{C_{maxim}^{1-\frac{2}{2q^{\prime}}}\cdot\bigg(\mathbb{E}\{m^{2}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}(s,a),\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}(s,a))\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg)^{\frac{2}{2q^{\prime}}}\bigg\}^{\delta}+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle=2C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{(1-\frac{1}{q^{\prime}})\delta}\cdot\bar{m}_{d_{\pi},1}^{\frac{1}{q}}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}.

If we have
q^{\prime}\leq 1
, then we have
\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2C_{surr}\cdot\bar{m}_{d_{\pi},1}^{\delta}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}
by Jensen’s inequality (even for
q^{\prime}<1
). Thus, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot\bar{m}_{d_{\pi},1}^{\delta^{\prime}}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}\quad\text{with }\ \delta^{\prime}=\min\{\delta,1/q\}.

(23)

Restoring the iteration index
t\in[T]
(i.e.,
\theta_{t}={\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}
), we have

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}})\leq C_{1}\cdot O_{P}(n^{\frac{-\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}})+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0},\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle C_{1}:=C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\cdot\bigg(C_{subg}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}}{1-\alpha/2}\bigg)^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{l-1+\alpha/2}}.

Recall that the data (which are independently collected) are split into non-overlapping subdatasets
\mathcal{D}=\cup_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{t}
(see Section
3
). This ensures that
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
can be considered as a given constant at each
t
-th iteration.

Lemma C.6
.

With
X_{n,t}=\tilde{C}\cdot O_{P}(n^{-1/b})
for some
b>0
which can be possibly dependent for different
t\in\mathbb{N}
, assuming
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}|n^{1/b}\cdot X_{n,t}|<\infty
, then we have
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot X_{n,t}=\tilde{C}\cdot O_{P}(\frac{1}{1-\zeta}\cdot n^{-1/b})
. See Appendix
D.2
for proof.

In our case, letting
X_{n,t}=\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}})
(where
n
affects
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}{\hat{F}_{n,t}}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
) and
\tilde{C}=C_{1}
, we can show that
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}|n^{1/b}\cdot X_{n,t}|<\infty
is satisfied. Since its proof requires redundant usage of techniques that we have already employed, we will defer the proof to Appendix
C.6.3
for the sake of simplicity.

After applying Lemma
C.6
, let us use (
6
) with
n=N/T
. Letting
T=\lfloor\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}\cdot\log_{1/\gamma}N\rfloor
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})\leq\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}}\cdot C_{0}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{N}{\log_{1/\gamma}N}\bigg)^{\frac{-\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}}\bigg\}+\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}}\cdot 2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0},

where

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle:=C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\times

(24)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\bigg\{C_{subg}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}}{1-\alpha/2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg\}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{l-1+\alpha/2}},

with
C_{maxim}:=\sup_{\theta,{\tilde{\theta}}\in\Theta}m_{\infty}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\tilde{\theta}})
. For
C_{maxim}=\infty
, we let
C_{maxim}^{0}=1
. This validates Theorem
C.4
.

C.6.3
Satisfaction of the condition of Lemma
C.6
in Theorem
C.4

Stage-1: Apply peeling argument

Arbitrarily choose an integer
M\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence
(\delta_{n})_{\geq 1}
. we can use the logic of standard proof of convergence in M-estimation (e.g., Section 5.1 of
[
48
]
). Define
\mathcal{Z}_{t}:=\{(s_{i},a_{i})\ |\ (s_{i},a_{i},r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}\}
and
S_{n,j}:=\{\theta\in\Theta:2^{j-1}\cdot\delta_{n}<\bar{m}_{n,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2^{j}\cdot\delta_{n}\}
, and we can obtain the following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})>2^{M-1}\cdot\delta_{n}\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg)=\sum_{j\geq M}\mathbb{P}\bigg(2^{j-1}\cdot\delta_{n}<\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2^{j}\cdot\delta_{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq M}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\Big\{-{\hat{F}_{n,t}}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})+{\hat{F}_{n,t}}({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\Big\}\geq 0\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq M}\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{\bar{m}_{n,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2^{j}\cdot\delta_{n}}\Big|\Delta_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\Big|\geq\lambda\cdot 2^{l(j-1)}\cdot\delta_{n}^{l}\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg)\quad\text{by (NS2)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq M}\frac{1}{\lambda\cdot 2^{l(j-1)}\cdot\delta_{n}^{l}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\sup_{\bar{m}_{n,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2^{j}\cdot\delta_{n}}\Big|\Delta_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\Big|\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\lambda}\cdot\frac{C_{subg}}{2^{-l}}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}}{1-\alpha/2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\delta_{n}^{1-\alpha/2-l}\cdot\sum_{j\geq M}(2^{j})^{1-\alpha/2-l}\quad\text{by (NS4) }

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{C}{\lambda}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot C_{brack}^{1/2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}^{-\alpha}}{1-\alpha/2}\cdot\frac{2^{l}}{1-2^{1-\alpha/2-l}}}_{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}C_{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot(2^{M}\cdot\delta_{n})^{1-\alpha/2-l}

\displaystyle=C_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot(2^{M}\cdot\delta_{n})^{1-\alpha/2-l}.

For arbitrary
\epsilon>0
, we can choose
M\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta_{n}>0
be such that
2^{M}\cdot\delta_{n}=\epsilon
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}}|\mathcal{Z}_{t})>\epsilon)\leq C_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\epsilon^{1-\alpha/2-l}.

Now letting
\epsilon^{1-\alpha/2-l}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\tilde{\epsilon}
for arbitrary
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
, we have following with
b=2(l-1)+\alpha>2
and
C_{2}>0
not depending on
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
(and thereby
{\theta_{*,t}}
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(n^{1/b}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})>\tilde{\epsilon}\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg)\leq C_{2}\cdot\tilde{\epsilon}^{-b/2}.

Based on the above tail probability, we can bound the expectation as follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{n^{1/b}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg\}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigg(n^{1/b}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\geq t\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=1+\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigg(n^{1/b}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\geq t\bigg|\mathcal{Z}_{t}\bigg)\mathrm{d}t\leq 1+\frac{C_{2}}{b/2-1}.

(25)

Note that the bound does not depend on
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
.

Stage-2: Convert the
\bar{m}_{n,1}
-bound into
\bar{m}_{\rho,1}
-bound

First fix
c\in(0,1)
. Based on Lemma 6.3.4 of
[
51
]
, we have
L_{c},\alpha_{c}>0
(that do not depend on
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
) such that following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{\|G_{\theta_{1}}-G_{\theta_{2}}\|_{m,\rho}\geq L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}}\bigg|\frac{\|G_{\theta_{1}}-G_{\theta_{2}}\|_{m,n}}{\|G_{\theta_{1}}-{\theta_{2}}\|_{m,\rho}}-1\bigg|\leq c\bigg)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\alpha_{c}}\cdot\exp(-\alpha_{c}\cdot L_{c}^{2}\cdot n^{\frac{b-2}{b}}).

Denoting the event as
\Omega_{E}
and note that this only regards to the observation of
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
and not the
r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime}\sim{\tilde{p}}(\cdots|s_{i},a_{i})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\}=\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\cdot 1\Big(\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\leq L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}\Big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\cdot 1\Big(\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})>L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}\Big)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\cdot 1\Big(\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})>L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}\Big)\bigg|\Omega_{E}\bigg\}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{E})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\cdot 1\Big(\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})>L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}\Big)\bigg|\Omega_{E}^{c}\bigg\}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{E}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq L_{c}\cdot n^{-1/b}+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\frac{1}{1-c}\cdot\bar{m}_{n,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\bigg|\Omega_{E}\bigg\}+C_{maxim}\cdot\exp(-\alpha_{c}\cdot L_{c}^{2}\cdot n^{\frac{b-2}{b}})

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\{L_{c}+\frac{1}{1-c}\cdot\bigg(1+\frac{C_{2}}{b/2-1}\bigg)+C_{3}\bigg\}\cdot n^{-1/b}.

The last line holds due to(
25
), since
\Omega_{E}
only regards to
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
. Also,
C_{3}>0
is some large value such that
C_{maxim}\cdot\exp(-\alpha_{c}\cdot L_{c}^{2}\cdot n^{\frac{b-2}{b}})\leq C_{3}\cdot n^{-1/b}
holds for sufficiently large
n
.

Stage-3: Bound the contractive metric

As we did in (
23
), we can derive

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\leq C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\delta^{\prime}/2}\cdot\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{\delta^{\prime}/2}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}

We switch
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}
with
\Upsilon_{\theta_{t}}
and
{\theta_{*,t}}
with
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}}
. Based on Jensen’s inequality, we obtain the following for all
t\in[T]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{t}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}})\bigg\}\leq C_{4}\cdot n^{\frac{-\delta^{\prime}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}}+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0},

where
C_{4}=C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\delta^{\prime}/2}\cdot(\frac{C_{2}}{b/2-1})^{\delta^{\prime}}
. Without loss of generality, we can assume
\epsilon_{0}=0
, since this will later add up to the non-converging term
2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}/(1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}})
in the final bound (Theorem
C.4
). Then, condition of Lemma
C.6
is satisfied with
\tilde{b}=\frac{2(l-1)+\alpha}{\delta^{\prime}}
.

C.7
About IPS metrics

C.7.1
How squared IPS metric becomes a functional Bregman divergence

Assume an IPS metric (Definition
C.13
) where
G(\cdot|P)
can be defined for dirac delta
P=\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
. This accommodates (i)
G(\cdot|P)
being cdf with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L_{2}}
and (ii)
G(\cdot|P)
being kernel mean embedding with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. See Appendix
C.5
for their detailed explanation. Define the score
S(P,\mathbf{x}):=-\|G(\cdot|P)-G(\cdot|\delta_{\mathbf{x}})\|_{m}^{2}
where
\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
denotes dirac delta at
\mathbf{x}
. Then we have

\displaystyle-\int S(P,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}Q(\mathbf{x})=\int\|G(\cdot|P)-G(\cdot|\delta_{\mathbf{x}})\|_{m}^{2}\mathrm{d}Q(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim Q}\bigg\{\|G(\cdot|P)\|_{m}^{2}+\|G(\cdot|\delta_{\mathbf{x}})\|_{m}^{2}-2\cdot\langle G(\cdot|P),G(\cdot|\delta_{\mathbf{x}})\rangle_{m}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\|G(\cdot|P)\|_{m}^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim Q}\|G(\cdot|\delta_{\mathbf{x}})\|_{m}^{2}-2\cdot\langle G(\cdot|P),G(\cdot|Q)\rangle_{m}

Since we have
-\int S(Q,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}Q(\mathbf{x})=-\|G(\cdot|Q)\|_{m}^{2}+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim Q}\|G(\cdot|\delta_{\mathbf{x}})\|_{m}^{2}
, this leads to a valid functional Bregman divergence as follows (based on equivalence stated before (
5
)),

\displaystyle m^{2}(P,Q)=\|G(\cdot|P)-G(\cdot|Q)\|_{m}^{2}=\int S(Q,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}Q(\mathbf{x})-\int S(P,\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}Q(\mathbf{x}).

Of course, there are cases where
G(\cdot|P)
does not exist for dirac delta’s, most representatively
G(\cdot|P)
being a pdf with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{m}
. However, this case is shown to make a functional Bregman divergence (Section 4.1 of
[
22
]
).

C.7.2
How IPS Properties imply NS Properties

Under (IPS1), (IPS2) of Definition
C.13
, and (
11
), we shall show
(\mathfrak{d},m)
with
\mathfrak{d}=m^{2}
satisfies NS Properties of Section
3.2
with
l=2
,
\lambda=1
, and
C_{subg}=C_{max,m}
.

(NS1) can be shown as follows. With
A_{i}^{\theta}=m^{2}(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Psi_{\pi}(R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}))
where
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon):=\int_{\mathcal{A}}(g_{\gamma,r})_{\#}\Upsilon(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\pi(\mathrm{d}a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}
(due to convexity of
\mathcal{P}
, provided that
\mathcal{P}
satisfies the assumption of Theorem
2.4
),

\displaystyle\therefore\ |\Delta_{n}({\theta_{1}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-\Delta_{n}({\theta_{2}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})|=\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{A_{i}^{\theta_{1}}-\mathbb{E}(A_{i}^{\theta_{1}})\}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{A_{i}^{\theta_{2}}-\mathbb{E}(A_{i}^{\theta_{2}})\}\bigg|.

Since we have
\|\Delta_{n}({\theta_{1}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-\Delta_{n}({\theta_{2}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\|_{\psi_{2}(n)}^{2}\leq C\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|A_{i}^{\theta_{1}}-A_{i}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}(n)}^{2}
by Proposition 2.6.1 and Lemma 2.6.8 of
[
55
]
, we shall bound
|A_{i}^{\theta_{1}}-A_{i}^{\theta_{2}}|
.

\displaystyle|A_{i}^{\theta_{1}}-A_{i}^{\theta_{2}}|

\displaystyle=\bigg|m^{2}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Psi_{\pi}(r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\bigg\}-m^{2}\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Psi_{\pi}(r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\bigg\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot C_{max,m}\cdot m(\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s_{i},a_{i})).

Here,
C_{max,m}>0
is a constant (that depends on the corresponding kernel
k
) such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta,{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}\sup_{r,s^{\prime}}\bigg\{m(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}))\bigg\}\leq C_{max,m},

\displaystyle\text{For }m={\rm MMD}\text{ bounded kernel }k(\cdot,\cdot)\text{, we can let }C_{max,m}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}C\cdot\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}k^{1/2}(x,y)

(26)

Thus we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{n}({\theta_{1}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-\Delta_{n}({\theta_{2}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\|_{\psi_{2}(n)}\leq\frac{C_{subg}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\overline{m}_{n,1}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}})\quad\text{with}\quad C_{subg}=C\cdot C_{max,m}.

(NS2) can be shown as follows,

\displaystyle F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{m^{2}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Psi_{\pi}(R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big)-m^{2}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Psi_{\pi}(R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big)\bigg\}

The terms within the curly bracket can be rewritten as follows with temporarily letting
\Psi_{i}:=\Psi_{\pi}(R_{i},S_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
,

\displaystyle\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))-G(\cdot|\Psi_{i})\|_{m}^{2}-\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}))-G(\cdot|\Psi_{i})\|_{m}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))\|_{m}^{2}-\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}))\|_{m}^{2}-2\cdot\langle G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))-G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i})),G(\cdot|\Psi_{i})\rangle_{m}.

Taking its expectation so that
\mathbb{E}(G(\cdot|\Psi))=G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}))
, we have
\lambda=1
as follows,

\displaystyle F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))\|_{m}^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}))\|_{m}^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))-G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i})),G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))-G(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i}))\|_{m}^{2}=\bar{m}_{n,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}}).

(NS3) can be shown as follows. Let us define the following,
