H_{1}(\cdot)\in[l_{j_{1}}^{(1)},u_{j_{1}}^{(1)}],\cdots,H_{K}(\cdot)\in[l_{j_{K}}^{(K)},u_{j_{K}}^{(K)}]
for some
j_{1},\cdots,j_{K}\in[M]
. Then, letting
F_{l}(\cdot|s,a)=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}(s,a)l_{j_{k}}^{(k)}(\cdot)
and
F_{u}(\cdot|s,a)=\sum_{k}\phi_{k}(s,a)u_{j_{k}}^{(k)}(\cdot)
, we have
F(\cdot|\cdots)\in[F_{l}(\cdot|\cdots),F_{u}(\cdot|\cdots)]
. We eventually have

\displaystyle\|F_{l}(\cdot|\cdots)

\displaystyle-F_{u}(\cdot|\cdots)\|_{L_{1},\infty}=\sup_{s,a}\int_{a}^{b}\bigg|F_{u}(z|s,a)-F_{l}(z|s,a)\bigg|\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{s,a}\int_{a}^{b}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi_{k}(s,a)\cdot\bigg|u_{j_{k}}^{(k)}(z)-l_{j_{k}}^{(k)}(z)\bigg|\mathrm{d}z\leq\sup_{k\in[K]}\|u_{j_{k}}-l_{j_{k}}\|_{L_{1}}\leq\epsilon,

which leads to
\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty},\epsilon)\leq\{\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1}},\epsilon)\}^{K}
. Note that we have
\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\sup_{s,a}\sup_{z}|F_{\theta}(z|s,a)|\leq 1
and can let
C_{brack}=CK(b-a)
,
\alpha=1
in (NS4). Eventually this leads to
\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{-1/(3p)})

\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}
-convergence in Corollary
B.1
. However, FLE cannot suggest a meaningful bound since they assume the existence of conditional densities, which may not hold in our cdf-based model.

C.17.3
Example 2: Linear Quadratic Regulator

We will denote state-action pairs as
x,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}}
, its conditional reward as
R(x,a)
, and subsequent stater as
x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}(x,a)
. We assume the following setting

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(x,a)=Ax+Ba\quad\&\quad R(x,a)=x^{\intercal}Qx+a^{\intercal}Ra+{\epsilon_{R}}\quad\&\quad\pi(x)=Kx,

\displaystyle\text{where }{\epsilon_{R}}\text{ can follow any fixed distribution. }

(38)

Temporarily, we will assume bounded distribution for
{\epsilon_{R}}
(although this can be relaxed for Energy FDE). Letting
{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}:=\sum_{t\geq 1}\gamma^{t-1}\epsilon_{R,t}
with
\epsilon_{R,t}
being iid copies of
{\epsilon_{R}}
, we assume the following model,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\Theta}:=\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}\in(\mathcal{P}[a,b])^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\quad\text{where }\Upsilon_{\theta}(x,a)\text{ is the probability for }Z_{\theta}(x,a)=

(39)

\displaystyle x^{\intercal}M_{1}x+a^{\intercal}M_{2}x+a^{\intercal}M_{3}a+{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}\quad\text{with }\theta=(M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}\times d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}\times d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}\times d_{a}}\bigg\}.

Throughout the proof, we shall assume the analogous statements that
[
61
]
suggested in their Lemma B.3, bounding
\|x\|,\|a\|,\|M_{1}\|_{F},\|M_{2}\|_{F},\|M_{3}\|_{F}
.

Assumption
3.1
can be satisfied with appropriate modeling of
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}\times d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}\times d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}\times d_{a}}
. By following the same logic shown in D.9 of
[
61
]
, we can present an example. Skipping all calculations,
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}Z_{\theta}(x,a)\sim\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}(x,a)
follows a distribution
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}Z_{\theta}(x,a)=\mathbb{E}\{\mathcal{T}^{\pi}Z_{\theta}(x,a)\}+{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}
with,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\mathcal{T}^{\pi}Z_{\theta}(x,a)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=x^{\intercal}\bigg\{Q+\gamma(A^{\intercal}M_{1}A+A^{\intercal}K^{\intercal}M_{2}A+A^{\intercal}K^{\intercal}M_{3}KA)\bigg\}x

\displaystyle+x^{\intercal}\bigg\{\gamma\cdot\bigg(A^{\intercal}(M_{1}+M_{1}^{\intercal})B+A^{\intercal}(K^{\intercal}M_{2}+M_{2}^{\intercal}K)B+A^{\intercal}K^{\intercal}(M_{3}+M_{3}^{\intercal})KB\bigg)\bigg\}a

\displaystyle+a^{\intercal}\bigg\{R+\gamma\cdot\bigg(B_{1}^{\intercal}M_{1}B+B^{\intercal}K^{\intercal}M_{2}B+B^{\intercal}K^{\intercal}M_{3}KB\bigg)\bigg\}a

Let our model be
\Theta=\{(M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}):\|M\|_{F},\|M_{2}\|_{F},\|M_{3}\|_{F}\leq m\}
,
\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta}\in\mathcal{M}_{\Theta}
holds if the following conditions are satisfied,

\displaystyle\|Q\|_{F}+\gamma\cdot\|A\|_{F}^{2}\cdot(1+\|K\|_{F}+\|K\|_{F}^{2})\cdot m\leq m

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot\|A\|_{F}\cdot\|B\|_{F}\cdot\big\{1+2\|K\|_{F}+\|K\|_{F}^{2}\big\}\cdot 2m\leq m

\displaystyle\|R\|_{F}+\gamma\cdot\|B\|_{F}^{2}\cdot\big\{1+\|K\|_{F}+\|K\|_{F}^{2}\big\}\cdot m\leq m.

Assumption
C.3
can be simplified as follows. For
X=\mu_{x}+{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}
and
Y=\mu_{y}+{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}
, we have the following with
P_{X},P_{Y}
being their probability measures and
F_{X},F_{Y}
being their cdf’s,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{1}(P_{X},P_{Y})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|F_{X}(z)-F_{Y}(z)|\mathrm{d}z=|\mu_{y}-\mu_{x}|\cdot 1=|\mu_{y}-\mu_{x}|.

Note that the second equality holds, since
F_{Y}
is only a location shift of
F_{X}
, i.e.,
F_{Y}(\cdot)=F_{X}(\cdot-(\mu_{y}-\mu_{x}))
. This leads to the following with
\theta_{A}=(M_{1}^{(A)},M_{2}^{(A)},M_{3}^{(A)})
and
\theta_{B}=(M_{1}^{(B)},M_{2}^{(B)},M_{3}^{(B)})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{1}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{A}}(x,a),\Upsilon_{\theta_{B}}(x,a)\big\}

\displaystyle=x^{\intercal}(M_{1}^{(A)}-M_{1}^{(B)})x+a^{\intercal}(M_{2}^{(A)}-M_{2}^{(B)})x+a^{\intercal}(M_{3}^{(A)}-M_{3}^{(B)})a

\displaystyle=\langle{\bm{\phi}}(x,a),\bm{\theta}_{AB}\rangle,

where
{\bm{\phi}}(x,a):=(x^{\intercal},a^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\otimes(x^{\intercal},a^{\intercal})^{\intercal}
and some
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{x}+d_{a})^{2}}
. Using the fact that
\mathbb{W}_{1}\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{A}}(x,a),\Upsilon_{\theta_{B}}(x,a)\}=\bm{\theta}_{AB}^{\intercal}{\bm{\phi}}(x,a){\bm{\phi}}(x,a)^{\intercal}\bm{\theta}_{AB}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{{\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}\in\Theta}\frac{\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}^{2}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}})}{\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,\rho,1}^{2}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}})}\leq\sup_{\bm{\theta}_{AB}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d_{x}+d_{a})^{2}},\bm{\theta}_{AB}\neq\mathbf{0}}\frac{\bm{\theta}_{AB}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{{\bm{\phi}},d_{\pi}}\bm{\theta}_{AB}}{\bm{\theta}_{AB}^{\intercal}\Sigma_{{\bm{\phi}},\rho}\bm{\theta}_{AB}},

\displaystyle\text{where }\quad\Sigma_{{\bm{\phi}},d_{\pi}}:=\mathbb{E}_{x,a\sim d_{\pi}}\big\{{\bm{\phi}}(x,a){\bm{\phi}}(x,a)^{\intercal}\big\}\quad\&\quad\Sigma_{{\bm{\phi}},\rho}:=\mathbb{E}_{x,a\sim\rho}\big\{{\bm{\phi}}(x,a){\bm{\phi}}(x,a)^{\intercal}\big\}.

This is the same result that
[
61
]
provided in their Lemma B.2.

Lastly, let us bound the term
\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{\infty,1},\epsilon)
for (NS4). First, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu}:=\bigg\{\mu_{\theta}:\mathcal{S\times A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ \bigg|\ \mu_{\theta}(x,a)=\mathbb{E}_{Z_{\theta}(x,a)\sim\Upsilon_{\theta}(x,a)}\big\{Z_{\theta}(x,a)\big\}\bigg\},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad\|\mu_{\theta_{1}}-\mu_{\theta_{2}}\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x,a\in\mathcal{S\times A}}\bigg|\mu_{\theta_{1}}(x,a)-\mu_{\theta_{2}}(x,a)\bigg|

We can see
\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty},\epsilon)\leq\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu},\|\cdot\|_{\infty},\epsilon)
for the following reason. Letting
M=\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu},\|\cdot\|_{\infty},\epsilon)
, there exist bracketing pairs
\{[l_{i}(\cdot),u_{i}(\cdot)]\}_{i=1}^{M}
such that

\displaystyle\forall\mu_{\theta}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu}\quad\exists i\in[M]\quad\text{such that}\quad\|l_{i}(\cdot)-u_{i}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon.

Now consider the bracketing pairs
[F_{l_{i}}(\cdot|\cdots),F_{u_{i}}(\cdot|\cdots)]\ (i=1,\cdots,M)
. For an arbitrary
F_{\theta}(\cdot|\cdots)\in\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}
, its corresponding expectation function satisfies
l_{i}(x,a)\leq\mu_{\theta}(x,a)\leq u_{i}(x,a)
for some
i\in[M]
. As we have previously mentioned, all
F_{\theta}(\cdot|x,a)
are results of location-shifts of the cdf of
{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}
. This leads to
F_{\theta}(\cdot|\cdots)\in[F_{l_{i}}(\cdot|\cdots),F_{u_{i}}(\cdot|\cdots)]
for some
i\in[M]
, and it satisfies

\displaystyle\|F_{l_{i}}(\cdot|\cdots)-F_{u_{i}}(\cdot|\cdots)\|_{L_{1},\infty}=\|l_{i}(\cdot)-u_{i}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon.

Thus, we have
\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty},\epsilon)\leq\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu},\|\cdot\|_{\infty},\epsilon)
. Further assuming
\|x\|\leq D_{x}
and
\|a\|\leq D_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mu_{\theta_{A}}

\displaystyle-\mu_{\theta_{B}}\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x,a}\bigg|x^{\intercal}(M_{1}^{(A)}-M_{2}^{(B)})x+a^{\intercal}(M_{2}^{(A)}-M_{2}^{(B)})x+a^{\intercal}(M_{3}^{(A)}-M_{3}^{(B)})a\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\max\{D_{x},D_{a}\}^{2}\cdot\bigg\{\|M_{1}^{(A)}-M_{1}^{(B)}\|_{op}+\|M_{2}^{(A)}-M_{2}^{(B)}\|_{op}+\|M_{3}^{(A)}-M_{3}^{(B)}\|_{op}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq 3D_{0}^{2}\cdot d_{3}(\theta_{A},\theta_{B}),

where
d_{3}(\theta_{A},\theta_{B}):=\max\{\|M_{1}^{(A)}-M_{1}^{(B)}\|_{F},\|M_{2}^{(A)}-M_{2}^{(B)}\|_{F},\|M_{3}^{(A)}-M_{3}^{(B)}\|_{F}\}
and
D_{0}:=\max\{D_{x},D_{a}\}
. Then, we can bound our target as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty},\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu},\|\cdot\|_{\infty},\epsilon)=\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}^{\mu},\|\cdot\|_{\infty},\frac{\epsilon}{2})\leq\mathcal{N}(\Theta,d_{3},\frac{\epsilon}{6D_{0}^{2}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{N}(\Theta_{1},\|\cdot\|_{F},\frac{\epsilon}{6D_{0}^{2}})\times\mathcal{N}(\Theta_{2},\|\cdot\|_{F},\frac{\epsilon}{6D_{0}^{2}})\times\mathcal{N}(\Theta_{3},\|\cdot\|_{F},\frac{\epsilon}{6D_{0}^{2}}),

where
\Theta_{i}:=\{M_{i}:\|M_{i}\|_{F}\leq m\}
. Since
\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2},\Theta_{3}
with
\|\cdot\|_{F}
can be perceived as subsets of Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}d_{x}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}d_{x}},\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}d_{a}}
with Euclidean norms
\|\cdot\|
, we can use volume comparison lemma

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}(A,\|\cdot\|,\epsilon)\leq p\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{3\cdot{\rm diam}(A,\|\cdot\|)}{\epsilon}\bigg)\quad\text{for }A\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\quad\forall\epsilon\in(0,{\rm diam}(A,\|\cdot\|)).

This leads to the following,

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty,},\epsilon)\leq d_{x}^{3}d_{a}^{3}\cdot\log\bigg(\frac{18\cdot D_{0}^{2}\cdot m}{\epsilon}\bigg).

Since our term can be bounded by inverse of polynomial
\mathcal{N}_{[\ ]}(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{L_{1},\infty},\epsilon)\lesssim\epsilon^{-\alpha}
with any
\alpha>0
, we can let
\alpha_{N}=1/\log N
.

If we assume bounded distributions of
\epsilon_{\rm rew}
, then we can apply Corollary
B.1
with the same trick (
29
), Cramer FDE obtains
\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{-1/(2p)})
of
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{p,d_{\pi},p}
-convergence. If we assume unbounded distributions, then Energy FDE obtain
\tilde{O}_{P}(N^{-1/8})
of
\mathbb{W}_{1,d_{\pi},1}
-inaccuracy by Corollary
B.3
. For FLE
[
61
]
, unless we have a standard parametric distribution for
{\epsilon_{\rm ret}}
(e.g., truncated gaussian), we cannot obtain the bound, since we cannot relate
\|f_{\theta_{A}}(\cdot|\cdots)-f_{\theta_{B}}(\cdot|\cdots)\|_{L_{1},\infty}
and
d_{3}(\theta_{A},\theta_{B})
.

Appendix D
Auxilary proofs

D.1
Supporting theorem

Following is a standard convergence theorem in M-estimation. We slightly adapted it from Theorem 6.1 of
[
48
]
, only modifying
l=2
into generalized
l\geq 2
. So we will skip the proof.

Theorem D.1
.

(Adapted version of Theorem 6.1 of
[
48
]
) Assume that
F_{n,t}(\cdot):\Theta\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
has a minimizer
{\theta_{*}}
. Let
\tilde{\delta}_{n}\geq 0
be a sequence that satisfies the following for
\delta\geq\tilde{\delta}_{n}
:

1.

If
d_{n}(\theta,{\theta_{*}})\in(\frac{\delta}{2},\delta]
, then
F_{n,t}(\theta)-F_{n,t}({\theta_{*}})\geq\lambda\cdot\delta^{l}
.

2.

We have
\phi(\delta)=C_{0}\cdot\delta^{\alpha_{0}}
with some
\alpha_{0}\in(0,l)
such that following holds,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Theta:d_{n}(\theta,{\theta_{*}})\leq\delta}\bigg|\big\{{\hat{F}_{n,t}}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big\}-\big\{{\hat{F}_{n,t}}({\theta_{*}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F_{n,t}({\theta_{*}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big\}\bigg|\bigg]\leq\frac{\phi(\delta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Further assume that we have
\delta_{n}
and some estimator such that

\displaystyle\phi(\delta_{n})\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot\delta_{n}^{l}\quad\&\quad\delta_{n}\geq\tilde{\delta}_{n}\quad\&\quad{\hat{\theta}_{n}}\rightarrow^{P}{\theta_{*}}\text{ in }d_{n}.

Then, we have
d_{n}({\hat{\theta}_{n}},{\theta_{*}})=O_{P}(\delta_{n})
. If
\tilde{\delta}_{n}=0
, then we have
\delta_{n}=C_{0}^{\frac{1}{l-\alpha_{0}}}\cdot n^{\frac{-1}{2(l-\alpha_{0})}}
.

D.2
Proof of Lemma
C.6

Without loss of generality, we can assume
\tilde{C}=1
. We let
Z_{n,t}:=n^{1/b}\cdot X_{n,t}
, and
C=\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}|n^{1/b}\cdot X_{n,t}|<\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot Z_{n,t}\geq\epsilon\bigg)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot Z_{n,t}|}{\epsilon}\leq\frac{1}{1-\zeta}\cdot\frac{1}{\epsilon}\cdot\sup_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}|Z_{n,t}|.

Then, letting
\epsilon=\frac{C}{1-\zeta}\cdot\tilde{\epsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot Z_{n,t}\geq\frac{C}{1-\zeta}\cdot\tilde{\epsilon}\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{\tilde{\epsilon}}.

Thus we have
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot X_{n,t}=O_{P}(\frac{1}{1-\zeta}\cdot n^{-1/b})
. More generally, we have
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\zeta^{T-t}\cdot X_{n,t}=\tilde{C}\cdot O_{P}(\frac{1}{1-\zeta}\cdot n^{-1/b})
.

D.3
Proof of Lemma
C.7

Showing the upper bound is easy. Let
X,X^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{iid}}Q
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{iid}}P
. Based on technique shown in Appendix A.6 of
[
25
]
, we can derive

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\big\{\mathbb{E}\|X-Y\|-\mathbb{E}\|X-X^{\prime}\|\big\}+\big\{\mathbb{E}\|X-Y\|-\mathbb{E}\|Y-Y^{\prime}\|\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{W}_{1}(P,Q)+\mathbb{W}_{1}(P,Q)=2\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1}(P,Q).

Due to Jensen’s inequality, it is straightforward to see
\mathbb{W}_{p}(P,Q)\leq\mathbb{W}_{r}(P,Q)
for
p<r
.

Now let us show the lower bound. We assume that
X\sim P
and
Y\sim Q
have bounded support
[a,b]
, and we denote their cdf’s as
F_{P}
and
F_{Q}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{1}(P,Q)

\displaystyle=(b-a)\cdot\|F_{P}(U)-F_{Q}(U)\|_{L_{1}(\mathbb{P})}\quad\text{where }\|X\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{P})}:=(\mathbb{E}|X|^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}\ \ \&\ \ U\sim{\rm Unif}[a,b]

\displaystyle\leq(b-a)\cdot\|F_{P}(U)-F_{Q}(U)\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P})}\quad\text{by Jensen's inequality}

\displaystyle=(b-a)^{1/2}\cdot l_{2}(P,Q),

where
l_{2}^{2}(P,Q):=\int_{\infty}^{\infty}|F_{P}(x)-F_{Q}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x
denotes Cramer distance. Since Energy distance is equivalent to Cramer distance in 1D domain
[
6
]
, that is
\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}(P,Q)=l_{2}^{2}(P,Q)
, this leads to

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(P,Q)=2\cdot l_{2}^{2}(P,Q)\geq\frac{2}{b-a}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{1}^{2}(P,Q).

Using
\mathbb{W}_{p}^{p}(P,Q)\geq=(b-a)^{p-r}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{r}^{r}(P,Q)
that was shown by
[
42
]
(see their Section 2.3), we can show the lower bound.

D.4
Proof of Lemma
C.10

D.4.1
Conditioned event

Let
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}\in\Theta
be fixed. Then, we have a unique value of
{\theta_{*,t}}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
. Based on this, we define the following:

\displaystyle X_{i}^{\theta}:=m^{2}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i})\big\}\quad\text{where }s_{i},a_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits^{iid}}\rho,\quad\&\quad Y_{i}^{\theta}:=X_{i}^{\theta}-\mathbb{E}(X_{i}^{\theta}),

\displaystyle{\Theta_{\delta}}=N_{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}}({\theta_{*,t}},\delta):=\big\{\theta\in\Theta\ :\ \bar{m}_{\rho,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})<\delta\big\}.

With
\Omega
being the probability space, we define the following event based on
\beta\in(0,2]
whose exact value will later be determined,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\delta}

\displaystyle:=\bigg\{\omega\in\Omega\ :\ {\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\theta}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\theta})\bigg|\leq\delta^{\beta}\bigg\}

(40)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\omega\in\Omega\ :\ {\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{\theta}(\omega)-Y_{i}^{\theta_{*,t}}(\omega)\bigg|\leq\delta^{\beta}\bigg\}\quad(\because\ Y_{i}^{\theta_{*,t}}=0).

Prior to calculating its probability
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\delta})
, let us first show its sub-gaussianity. Since
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{2}}
is a norm (Example 2.5.7 of
[
55
]
), we can use its convexity to derive

\displaystyle\|Y_{1}^{\theta_{1}}-Y_{1}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq\|X_{1}^{\theta_{1}}-X_{1}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}}+\mathbb{E}\|X_{1}^{\theta_{1}}-X_{1}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}}.

Assume that
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in{\Theta_{\delta}}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle|X_{1}^{\theta_{1}}-X_{1}^{\theta_{2}}|

\displaystyle=\bigg|m^{2}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s_{1},a_{1}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{1},a_{1})\big\}-m^{2}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s_{1},a_{1}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{1},a_{1})\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|m\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s_{1},a_{1}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{1},a_{1})\big\}-m\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s_{1},a_{1}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{1},a_{1})\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\bigg|m\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s_{1},a_{1}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{1},a_{1})\big\}+m\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s_{1},a_{1}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{1},a_{1})\big\}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot{\rm diam}({\Theta_{\delta}};m_{\infty})\cdot m_{\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),

where
{\rm diam}({\Theta_{\delta}};m_{\infty}):=\sup_{{\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}\in{\Theta_{\delta}}}m_{\infty}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}})
. This leads to
\|Y_{1}^{\theta_{1}}-Y_{1}^{\theta_{2}}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C\cdot{\rm diam}({\Theta_{\delta}};m_{\infty})\cdot m_{\infty}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
. Then, by applying Theorem 8.1.6 of
[
55
]
, we have the following bound for arbitrary
u>0
,

\displaystyle{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}^{\theta}-Y_{i}^{\theta_{*,t}})\bigg|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot{\rm diam}({\Theta_{\delta}};m_{\infty})\cdot\bigg\{\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}({\Theta_{\delta}},m_{\infty},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon+u\cdot{\rm diam}({\Theta_{\delta}};m_{\infty})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\text{with probability larger than }1-2\cdot\exp(-u^{2}).

Using the constants defined in Lemma
C.10
, we can let
u=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\delta^{\beta}/C_{2}
to show that the following holds with probability larger than
1-2\cdot\exp(-n\cdot\delta^{2\beta}/4C_{2}^{2})
,

\displaystyle{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}^{\theta}-Y_{i}^{\theta_{*,t}})\bigg|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\beta}\quad\text{for }\forall\delta>0.

Now, we assume the following:

\displaystyle\text{Criterion-1: }\delta\geq\delta_{n}^{(1)}:=\bigg(\frac{2C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{1/\beta}.

(41)

Since we have
\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\beta}\geq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\delta})=\mathbb{P}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}^{\theta}-Y_{i}^{\theta_{*,t}})\bigg|\leq\delta^{\beta}\bigg\}\geq 1-2\cdot\exp(-n\cdot\delta^{2\beta}/4C_{2}^{2}).

(42)

Under
\Omega_{\delta}
, we have the following. Suppose
\theta\in{\Theta_{\delta}}
. In other words,
\bar{m}_{\rho,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})<\delta
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\big|\bar{m}_{n,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})-\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\big|=\bigg|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\theta}-\mathbb{E}(X_{1}^{\theta})\bigg|\leq\delta^{\beta},\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{n,1}({\theta_{1}},{\theta_{2}}):=\bigg[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m^{2}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta_{1}}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{2}}(s_{i},a_{i})\big\}\bigg]^{1/2}.

This leads to the following with
\beta^{\prime}=\min\{\beta,2\}
,

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{n,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})+\big|\bar{m}_{n,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})-\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\big|\leq\delta^{2}+\delta^{\beta}\leq 2\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}},

where we assumed
\delta\in(0,1)
without loss of generality. Then, we have the following,

\displaystyle\text{Under }\Omega_{\delta},\quad\text{for }\forall\theta\in{\Theta_{\delta}},\quad\bar{m}_{n,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}\ \text{holds}.

D.4.2
Concentration of modulus

Assume that
\mathcal{Z}_{t}:=\{(s_{i},a_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
is given (fixed). However, there still exists randomness in the transition
r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime}\sim{\tilde{p}}(\cdots|s_{i},a_{i})
. For this conditional probability, denote its corresponding conditional expectation as
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{t}}(\cdots):=\mathbb{E}(\cdots|\mathcal{Z}_{t})
and its corresponding sub-gaussian norm as
\|\cdot\|_{{\psi_{2}(n)}}
. Then, by Dudley’s inequality (Theorem 8.1.3 of
[
55
]
) and (MS1), we obtain following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{t}}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{t}}\bigg\{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta:\bar{m}_{n,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\bigg\}\quad\text{under }\Omega_{\delta}\ \eqref{conditionalevent_definition}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\cdot C_{subg}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(N_{\bar{m}_{n,1}}({\theta_{*,t}},\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}),\bar{m}_{n,1},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\cdot C_{subg}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(N_{\bar{m}_{n,1}}({\theta_{*,t}},\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}),\bar{m}_{n,1},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\cdot C_{subg}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,m_{\infty},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon.

By Assumption of Lemma
C.10
, we have
0<C_{3}<\infty
. Then, we have the following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\ \bigg|\ \Omega_{\delta}\bigg\}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\delta})+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\ \bigg|\ \Omega_{\delta}^{c}\bigg\}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\delta}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\ \bigg|\ \Omega_{\delta}\bigg\}+\mathbb{E}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\ \bigg|\ \Omega_{\delta}^{c}\bigg\}\cdot 2\exp\bigg(\frac{-n\cdot\delta^{2\beta}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\bigg)\quad\text{by \eqref{conditionalprobability}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\cdot C_{subg}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}\cdot\delta^{\beta^{\prime}/2}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\Theta,m_{\infty},\epsilon)}\mathrm{d}\epsilon+C_{3}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-n\cdot\delta^{2\beta}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\cdot C_{subg}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{\rm ent}\cdot\delta^{\frac{{\alpha_{0}}}{2}\beta^{\prime}}+C_{3}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-n\cdot\delta^{2\beta}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\bigg).

Then, we set another criterion as below,

\displaystyle\text{Criterion-2: }\delta\geq\delta_{n}^{(2)},\quad\text{where }\delta_{n}^{(2)}\text{ is such that }\delta>\delta_{n}^{(2)}\text{ implies}

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot C_{\rm ent}\cdot\delta^{\frac{{\alpha_{0}}}{2}\beta^{\prime}}\geq C_{3}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-n\cdot\delta^{2\beta}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\bigg).

(43)

This leads to following bound, whose RHS does not depend on
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg\{{\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{\delta}}}|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}|\bigg\}\leq\frac{2C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot C_{\rm ent}\cdot\delta^{\frac{{\alpha_{0}}}{2}\beta^{\prime}}.

(44)

D.5
Proof of Theorem
C.11

D.5.1
Main proof

In (MS2), we take expectation on both sides. Then, the LHS becomes
F(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
, where the unconditioned (not conditioned on
\mathcal{Z}_{t}
unlike (
19
)) objective function is defined as follows:

\displaystyle F(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}):=\mathbb{E}\bigg(\mathfrak{d}\big\{\Upsilon_{\theta}(S,A),\Psi_{\pi}(R,S^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\big\}\bigg)\quad\&\quad{\theta_{*,t}}=\arg\min_{\theta\in\Theta}F(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}).

For the RHS, we have following,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big(\bar{m}_{n,1}^{l}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\big)=\mathbb{E}\big\{\big(\bar{m}_{n,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\big)^{l/2}\big\}\geq\big\{\mathbb{E}\big(\bar{m}_{n,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\big)\big\}^{l/2}\quad\text{by Jensen's inequality}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\mathbb{E}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m^{2}\big(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}),\Upsilon_{\theta_{*,t}}(s_{i},a_{i})\big)\bigg)\bigg\}^{l/2}=\big\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{2}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\big\}^{l/2}=\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{l}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}}).

Thus we have
F(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})-F({\theta_{*,t}}|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})\geq\lambda\cdot\bar{m}_{\rho,1}^{l}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})
, by which we can replace (MS2).

Now, we shall combine this with (MS3) and Lemma
C.10
to apply Theorem
D.1
. We should find
\delta_{n}
and
\beta>0
that satisfies following with
\beta^{\prime}=\min\{\beta,2\}
,

\displaystyle C_{\phi}\cdot\delta_{n}^{\frac{{\alpha_{0}}}{2}\beta^{\prime}}\leq\sqrt{n}\cdot\delta_{n}^{l},\quad\delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\frac{2C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{1/\beta},\quad\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot C_{\rm ent}\cdot\delta_{n}^{\frac{{\alpha_{0}}}{2}\beta^{\prime}}\geq C_{3}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-n\cdot\delta_{n}^{2\beta}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\bigg).

(45)

The first condition of (
45
) can be rewritten as follows,

\displaystyle\delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\frac{C_{\phi}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l-{\alpha_{0}}\beta^{\prime}/2}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{requires to confirm }{\alpha_{0}}\beta^{\prime}<2l.

Remember that
\beta>0
is a free variable that we choose. There can be two cases: (i)
l\leq 2+{\alpha_{0}}
and (ii)
l>2+{\alpha_{0}}
. We will select
\beta
differently for each case (based on the first two conditions of (
45
)), and we will hereafter denote its selected value as
\beta_{0}
.

Let us consider the first case. To meet the first two conditions, we solve
l={\alpha_{0}}\beta^{\prime}/2=\beta
. Temporarily asuming
\beta\leq 2
(which also needs to be confirmed later), we obtain
\beta_{0}=l/(1+{\alpha_{0}}/2)
. We can confirm that
l\leq 2+{\alpha_{0}}
implies
\beta_{0}\leq 2
, and can see
{\alpha_{0}}\beta^{\prime}={\alpha_{0}}\beta_{0}<2l
.

In the second case, we let
\beta_{0}=2
. This leads to
\beta^{\prime}=2
, and the first two conditions of (
45
) can be summarized as follows,

\displaystyle\delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\frac{C_{\phi}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l-{\alpha_{0}}}}\quad\&\quad\delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\frac{2C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{1/2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\therefore\ \delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\frac{C_{\phi}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l-{\alpha_{0}}}}.

We confirm that
{\alpha_{0}}\beta^{\prime}=2{\alpha_{0}}<2(l-2)<2l
holds. Incorporating Cases (i) and (ii), we let

\displaystyle\beta_{0}=\min\bigg\{\frac{l}{1+{\alpha_{0}}/2},2\bigg\}\leq 2.

With the selected
\beta_{0}
, the first two conditions of (
45
) become following,

\displaystyle\text{Case (i) }l\leq 2+{\alpha_{0}}\ :\ \delta_{n}\geq\max\big\{C_{\phi},2C_{1}\big\}^{1/\beta_{0}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{1/\beta_{0}}\quad\text{with}\quad\beta_{0}=\frac{l}{1+{\alpha_{0}}/2},

\displaystyle\text{Case (ii) }l>2+{\alpha_{0}}\ :\ \delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\frac{C_{\phi}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{l-{\alpha_{0}}}}\quad\text{for sufficiently large }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Since
l\leq 2+{\alpha_{0}}
is equivalent to
l-{\alpha_{0}}\leq\frac{l}{1+{\alpha_{0}}/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{n}\geq\bigg(\max\{C_{\phi},2C_{1}\}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{1/\beta_{1}}\quad\text{with}\quad\beta_{1}=\max\bigg\{\frac{l}{1+{\alpha_{0}}/2},l-{\alpha_{0}}\bigg\}.

Now we select
\delta_{n}
as follows,

\displaystyle\delta_{n}=\underbrace{\bigg(\max\bigg\{C_{\phi},2C_{1},(2C_{2})^{\beta_{1}/\beta_{0}}\bigg\}\bigg)^{1/\beta_{1}}}_{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{let}}C_{A}}\cdot\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\bigg)^{1/\beta_{1}},

and we can confirm that third statement of (
45
) holds as follows. Note that the selected value of
\beta
is
\beta_{0}
, not
\beta_{1}
. Using some constant
C_{\rm LHS}>0
and
\beta_{0}\leq\beta_{1}
along with
C_{A}^{2\beta_{0}}\geq 4C_{2}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\text{LHS}=\frac{C_{\rm LHS}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\delta_{n}^{\frac{{\alpha_{0}}\beta_{0}}{2}}=\frac{C_{\rm LHS}}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{\beta_{1}}\cdot\frac{{\alpha_{0}}\beta_{0}}{2}}\geq C_{\rm LHS}\cdot(\log n)^{{\alpha_{0}}/4}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bigg)^{1+\frac{{\alpha_{0}}}{2}},

\displaystyle\text{RHS}=C_{3}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{-n\cdot\delta_{n}^{2\beta_{0}}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\bigg)=C_{3}\cdot\exp\bigg\{\frac{-n\cdot C_{A}^{2\beta_{0}}}{4C_{2}^{2}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\log n}{n}\bigg)^{\beta_{0}/\beta_{1}}\bigg\}\leq C_{3}\cdot\exp(-\log n)=\frac{C_{3}}{n}.

Since
{\alpha_{0}}\in(0,1)
as specified in Lemma
C.10
, we can see that LHS is larger than RHS for sufficiently large
n
, satisfying the third statement of (
45
).

Now we can apply peeling argument (Theorem
D.1
) to obtain the following,

\displaystyle\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})=O_{P}(\delta_{n})=C_{\phi}^{\frac{1}{l-1+\alpha}}\cdot C_{A}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{\log n}{n}\bigg)\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{2\beta_{1}}},

\displaystyle\therefore\ \bar{m}_{d_{\pi},1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})=C_{cover}^{1/2}\cdot\max\bigg\{C_{\phi},2C_{1},(2C_{2})^{\beta_{1}/\beta_{0}}\bigg\}^{1/\beta_{1}}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{\log n}{n}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2\beta_{1}}}\bigg\}.

Based on (
23
), we can obtain the following with
\delta^{\prime}=\min\{\delta,\frac{1}{q}\}
, where we restored the iteration index
t\in[T]
by letting
\theta_{t}={\hat{\theta}_{n,t}}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{t}}.\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}})\leq C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\cdot C_{B}^{\prime}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{\log n}{n}\bigg)^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}}\bigg\}+2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad C_{B}^{\prime}:=\max\bigg\{C_{\phi},2C_{1},(2C_{2})^{\beta_{1}/\beta_{0}}\bigg\}^{\delta^{\prime}/\beta_{1}}.

Then we shall use (
6
) based on Lemma
C.6
(with its satisfaction of condition deferred in Appendix
D.5.2
), with
n=N/T
by letting

\displaystyle T=\bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}\cdot\log_{1/\gamma}N\bigg\rfloor\quad\Rightarrow\quad\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\bigg)^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}}\approx\zeta^{T}.

Ignoring the flooring effect, this leads to

\displaystyle\frac{\log n}{n}=\frac{\log(N/T)}{N/T}\leq\frac{T}{N}\cdot\log N=\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\log(1/\gamma)}\cdot\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{N}.

Then, applying the same logic as Appendix
C.6.2
(extending towards multiple iterations), we finally obtain bound
\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})
by following,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}}\cdot C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\cdot C_{B}^{\prime}\cdot O_{P}\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{1}{\log(1/\gamma)}\cdot\frac{(\log N)^{2}}{N}\bigg)^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{1-\gamma^{c-\frac{1}{2q}}}\cdot 2^{1-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\epsilon_{0},

\displaystyle\text{with}\quad\beta_{1}=\max\bigg\{\frac{l}{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{4}\alpha},l-1+\frac{\alpha}{2}\bigg\}\quad\&\quad\beta_{0}=\min\bigg\{\frac{l}{\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{4}\alpha},2\bigg\},

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad C_{B}^{\prime}:=\max\bigg\{C_{\phi},2C_{1},(2C_{2})^{\beta_{1}/\beta_{0}}\bigg\}^{\delta^{\prime}/\beta_{1}}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}\bigg\}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2\beta_{1}}}.

(46)

where
C_{\phi}
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are defined in Lemma
C.10
.

D.5.2
Satisfaction of the condition of Lemma
C.6
in Theorem
C.11

Before bounding
\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{T}},\Upsilon_{\pi})
based on (
6
), we first apply Lemma
C.6
by letting
X_{n,t}=\bar{\eta}_{d_{\pi},q}(\Upsilon_{\theta_{t}},\mathcal{T}^{\pi}\Upsilon_{{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}})
with
\tilde{C}=C_{surr}\cdot C_{maxim}^{\max\{(1-\frac{1}{q\delta})\delta,0\}}\cdot C_{cover}^{\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}}\cdot C_{B}^{\prime}
. Proving
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}|n^{1/b}\cdot X_{n,t}|<\infty
is the analogous to Appendix
C.6.3
. We do not need Stage 2 of Appendix
C.6.3
. Skipping the details, we can repeat Stage 1 of Appendix
C.6.3
to obtain the following, with
{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}
defined in Lemma
C.10
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\geq 2^{M-1}\delta_{n}\bigg\}\leq\sum_{j\geq M}\frac{1}{\lambda\cdot 2^{l(j-1)}\cdot\delta_{n}^{l}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\sup_{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}(\theta,{\theta_{*,t}})\leq 2^{j}\cdot\delta_{n}}\Big|{\tilde{\Delta}_{n}(\theta|{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}\Big|\bigg\}.

Using (
44
) and letting
\beta^{\prime}=\min\{\frac{l}{1+\alpha_{0}/2},2\}
with
\alpha_{0}=1-\alpha/2
(as we chose within the proof of Theorem
C.11
of Appendix
D.5
), we can take up the above inequality as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg\{\bar{m}_{\rho,1}({\hat{\theta}_{n,t}},{\theta_{*,t}})\geq 2^{M-1}\delta_{n}\bigg\}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot C_{subg}\cdot C_{\rm ent}\cdot\frac{2^{l}}{1-2^{\frac{\alpha_{0}\beta^{\prime}}{2}-l}}\cdot(2^{M}\cdot\delta_{n})^{\frac{\alpha_{0}\beta^{\prime}}{2}-l}.

After that, copying the remaining logic of Stage-1 and Stage-2 of Appendix
C.6.3
will give us the desired result, i.e.,
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}|n^{1/b}\cdot X_{n,t}|<\infty
with
b=\delta^{\prime}/(2\beta_{1})
.

Appendix E
Experiment Details

E.1
Details in Linear Quadratic Regulator

LQR is a parametric environment with deterministic transitions and Gaussian noise in the rewards.
It can be verified that Assumption
C.3
holds given the chosen behavior policy, and Assumptions
3.1
is satisfied under the appropriate model space, both of which we will present.
We will compare the performance of the baseline method FLE
[
61
]
with our proposed FDE method using different functional Bregman divergences: Energy (
\beta=1
), Laplace (Matern with
\nu=1
), RBF, PDF-L2, KL. The evaluation is based on the
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}
-inaccuracy.

The proposed FDE methods using different functional Bregman divergences outperform FLE in terms of both mean inaccuracy and stability (as measured by standard deviation), as visualized in the inaccuracy graphs of Figure
2
.
This performance gap is expected, as FLE does not make use of the closed-form density of
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
, making it suffer from large Monte Carlo errors.

As for the comparison of the proposed FDE methods, we found it
align with the convergence rates of Table
2
. PDF-L2 and RBF (Corollary
B.6
) (with sufficiently small tuning parameter
{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}
) perform best, followed by Matern (or Laplace), Energy (which have slower rates) and KL (which is not theoretically guaranteed for unbounded distributions).
See Table
4
in Appendix
E.1.4
for detailed inaccuracy results.

E.1.1
Data collection and model

Our offline data are collected as follows. States
x=(r\cdot\cos\theta_{x},r\cdot\sin\theta_{x})^{\intercal}
are generated with
r\sim\text{Unif}[0,1]
,
\theta_{x}\sim\text{Unif}[0,2\pi]
. Given the state, action is generated by behavior policy
b(a|x)=1/5
with
a=\text{Rot}(\theta_{a})x
where
\text{Rot}(\theta_{a})
is the (counter-clockwise) rotation matrix with angle
\theta_{a}\sim\text{Unif}\{0,\frac{2\pi}{5},\frac{4\pi}{5},\frac{6\pi}{5},\frac{8\pi}{5}\}
.
Since the all
x
within the unit circle has positive density value and
\text{Rot}(\theta_{a})=K
holds with positive probability, Assumption
C.3
is satisfied.

We assume deterministic target policy (which, for the time being, can be regarded as
\pi:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
with abuse of notation). With Gaussian noise added to the reward
{\epsilon_{R}}\sim N(0,\sigma_{0}^{2})
, our goal is to estimate
\Upsilon_{\pi}\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S\times A}}
. States
x\in\mathcal{S}
and actions
a\in\mathcal{A}
are both generated from a continuous subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(i.e.,
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
). Matrices
A,B,Q,R\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
that control the environment dynamics (corresponding to
{\tilde{p}}(r,s^{\prime}|s,a)
) are as follows (but unknown in the simulations).

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(x,a)=Ax+Ba\quad\&\quad R(x,a)=x^{\intercal}Qx+a^{\intercal}Ra+{\epsilon_{R}}\quad\&\quad\pi(x)=Kx,

\displaystyle A=\begin{bmatrix}0.6&0\\
0&0.8\end{bmatrix}\quad\&\quad B=\begin{bmatrix}0.2&0\\
0&0.1\end{bmatrix}\quad\&\quad Q=\begin{bmatrix}4&1\\
1&4\end{bmatrix}\quad\&\quad R=\begin{bmatrix}2&1\\
1&2\end{bmatrix}.

Letting
K
be the identity matrix, we assumed
\gamma=0.99
and
\sigma_{0}=1
.

Return distribution model based on (
39
) of Appendix
C.17.3
can be written as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\Theta}:=\bigg\{\Upsilon_{\theta}\in(\mathcal{P}[a,b])^{\mathcal{S\times A}}\quad\text{where }\Upsilon_{\theta}(x,a)\text{ is the probability for }Z_{\theta}(x,a)=

\displaystyle x^{\intercal}M_{1}x+a^{\intercal}M_{2}x+a^{\intercal}M_{3}a+N\Big(0,\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{1-\gamma^{2}}\Big)\quad\text{with}\quad\theta=(M_{1},M_{2},M_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2\times 3}\bigg\}.

Note that this setup satisfies Assumption
3.1
(proof in Appendix
C.17.3
).

E.1.2
Data splitting

We applied the same splitting rule of data
\mathcal{D}=\cup_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{D}_{t}
by selecting the following
T
. We acknowledge that we did not strictly apply the rule of Theorem
C.4
that will lead to asymptotic convergence with the suggested rate.

1.

We used the rule of Theorem
C.4
with the parameters of Energy FDE with inaccuracy measured with Wasserstein-1 metric (
l=5,\delta=1,c=1,q=1
). We also used
\alpha=0
since it is a parametric model.

2.

We divided it with a constant
C_{divide}>0
and then choose the floored integer as follows. Since larger discount rate shall require bigger subdatasets (i.e., larger
|\mathcal{D}_{t}|
), we let
C_{divide}=5
for
\gamma=0.99
.

\displaystyle T=\bigg\lfloor\frac{1}{C_{divide}}\times\frac{1}{c-\frac{1}{2q}}\cdot\frac{\min\{\delta,1/q\}}{2(l-1)+\alpha}\cdot\log_{1/\gamma}N\bigg\rfloor

3.

Allocate the same number of samples into each of the
T
subdatasets, and then put all the remaining samples to the last one
\mathcal{D}_{T}
.

E.1.3
Closed form for gaussian distributions

Since we have gaussian conditional densities, we have used the following closed form. For MMD that we have used (Energy, Laplace, RBF), we have the following with
k(x,y)=k_{0}(x-y)
with mutually independent
X,X^{\prime}\sim P
and
Y,Y^{\prime}\sim Q
,

\displaystyle{\rm MMD}_{k}(P,Q):=\mathbb{E}\{k_{0}(X-X^{\prime})\}+\mathbb{E}\{k_{0}(Y-Y^{\prime})\}-2\cdot\mathbb{E}\{k_{0}(X-Y)\}.

Defining
K_{0}(\mu,\sigma^{2}):=\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim N(\mu,\sigma^{2})}\{k_{0}(Z)\}
, we have following for Energy distance which has
k_{0}(y)=-|y|
,

\displaystyle K_{0}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=-\left[\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)+|\mu|\left(1-2\Phi\left(-\frac{|\mu|}{\sigma}\right)\right)\right].

RBF kernel with
k_{0}(y)=\exp(-y^{2}/4{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2})
have the following.

\displaystyle K_{0}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\exp\left(-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}+2\sigma^{2}}\right)\bigg/\sqrt{1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}^{2}}}

Laplace kernel with
k_{0}(y)=\exp(-|y|/\sigma_{\rm Lap})
has

\displaystyle K_{0}(\mu,\sigma^{2})=\exp\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2{\sigma_{\rm Lap}}^{2}}-\frac{\mu}{\sigma_{\text{exp}}}\right)\cdot\Phi\left(\frac{\mu}{\sigma}-\frac{\sigma}{{\sigma_{\rm Lap}}}\right)+\exp\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2{\sigma_{\rm Lap}}^{2}}+\frac{\mu}{{\sigma_{\rm Lap}}}\right)\cdot\Phi\left(-\frac{\mu}{\sigma}-\frac{\sigma}{{\sigma_{\rm Lap}}}\right).

For PDF-L2, we used the following formula for
\phi(\cdot|\mu,\sigma^{2})
represents the density function of
N(\mu,\sigma^{2})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\phi(\cdot|\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})-\phi(\cdot|\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2})\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi\sigma_{1}^{2}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi\sigma_{2}^{2}}}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{2\pi(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})}}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{2}}{2(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2})}\right).

For KL Divergence, we have

\displaystyle\text{KL}(N(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})\parallel N(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2}))=\log\frac{\sigma_{2}}{\sigma_{1}}+\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}+(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{2}}{2\sigma_{2}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}.

Within our simulations, we have used fixed values of
{\sigma_{\rm Lap}}={\sigma_{\rm RBF}}=1
.

E.1.4
Simulation results

We have applied L-BFGS-B algorithm in solving the minimization problem of (
5
) at each iteration
t\in[T]
. For the initial value of L-BFGS-B algorithm, we always used the previous iterate
{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}
. Simulation results are visualized in Figure
2
, with details in Table
4
.

-inaccuracy (Y-axis: logarithmic scale) for different sample sizes
N
(X-axis) through 50 simulations. Shaded areas are
(\text{Mean}\pm\text{STD}/\sqrt{50})
regions for each method, with thick lines being the means.

E.2
Details in Atari games

E.2.1
Deep Neural Network structure

Our model consists of two parts: (i) CNN layers that reduce
4\times 84\times 84
image into
512
features, (ii) multiple layers that transform
512
features into
|\mathcal{A}|\times M\times 3
. Part (i) is identical to the structure of
[
33
]
that first applied DQN in Atari games. After training the optimal model via DQN (with the same network structure with
[
33
]
) for ourselves, we copied this part to (i), and then freezed it, assuming that this part already performs good recognition of the image. Part (ii) contains the components that we trained in our methods. This consists of multiple hidden layers that reduce the 512 features into
512\rightarrow 450\rightarrow 400\rightarrow 350\rightarrow 300\rightarrow 250\rightarrow 200\rightarrow 150\rightarrow 128\rightarrow|\mathcal{A}|\times(M\times 3)
, with each layer containing ReLU. For a given input image, the model outputs three parameters (weight, mean, variance) for each gaussian mixture component, for every action as follows,

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)=\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{m}(s,a;\theta)\cdot N(\mu_{m}(s,a;\theta),\sigma_{m}^{2}(s,a;\theta))\quad\text{where}\quad M=10,100,200.

Here,
w_{m}
represents gaussian component for the GMM model, which together sum up to 1, i.e.,
\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{m}(s,a;\theta)=1
.

For quantile-based methods, we preserve the same layers, except that the output distribution has
M
quantiles instead of
(M\times 3)
parameters. That is, this consists of multiple hidden layers that reduce the 512 features into
512\rightarrow 450\rightarrow 400\rightarrow 350\rightarrow 300\rightarrow 250\rightarrow 200\rightarrow 150\rightarrow 128\rightarrow|\mathcal{A}|\times M
, to form the following distributions based on dirac-delta’s
\delta_{m}
.

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\delta_{m}(s,a;\theta)\quad\text{where}\quad M=10,100,200.

E.2.2
Data collection, algorithmic details, inaccuracy measurement

We collected offline data (
N=2K,5K,10K
) through the trajectory of an agent following a behavior policy
b=\pi_{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}^{*}
(epsilon-greedy variant of the DQN-trained policy
\pi^{*}

[
33
]
) with
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.4(>0.3)
(so as to satisfy Assumption
C.3
). We applied techniques that are universally used in the literature (e.g., resampling, target network
[
33
]
).

Just like DQN
[
33
]
and early methods of distributional RL
[
4
,
14
, e.g.,]
, we set the target network which updates every 1000 iterations. Using the sample sizes
N=2K,5K,10K
, we ran 50K iterations, resampling a batch of 32 samples from the collected offline data in each iteration. We used Adam for the minimizing algorithm for all methods.

When measuring the inaccuracy, instead of
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})
that requires heavy computation to approximate, we computed
\mathbb{W}_{1}(\Upsilon_{\theta}^{d_{\pi}},\Upsilon_{\pi}^{d_{\pi}})
, where
\Upsilon^{d_{\pi}}:=\int_{\mathcal{S\times A}}\Upsilon(s,a)\mathrm{d}d_{\pi}(s,a)
, which can be bounded by
\mathbb{W}_{1}(\Upsilon_{\theta}^{d_{\pi}},\Upsilon_{\pi}^{d_{\pi}})\leq\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{1,d_{\pi},1}(\Upsilon_{\theta},\Upsilon_{\pi})
based on convexity of (
16
) in Appendix
C.4
. Here
d_{\pi}
is approximated with 1000 pre-sampled observations of state-action pairs.

E.2.3
Closed / approximated form

Based on the formula of Appendix
E.1.3
, we computed a single term in (
10
), i.e.,
\mathfrak{d}(\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a),\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}))
, for MMD methods and PDF-L2. Since Laplace FDE had ill performance due to its numerical difficulties that we aforementioned, we excluded it. For other methods (i.e., KL, TVD, Hyvarinen divergences), we do not have a closed form objective function for GMM. Therefore, we approximated it with Monte Carlo approximation by
z_{j_{2},b}^{\prime}\sim^{iid}N(r+\gamma\cdot\mu_{j_{2}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1}),\ \gamma^{2}\cdot\sigma_{j_{2}}^{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1}))
with
j_{2}=1,\cdots,M
and
b=1,\cdots,B
, which represents a single mixture component of
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
.

For KL FDE, we can convert it to maximizing the expected log-likelihood, with individual term being as follows. Here,
f(\cdot|P)
is the density of the probability measure
P
and
\phi(\cdot|\mu,\sigma^{2})
means the density of
N(\mu,\sigma^{2})
. When computing the density of
\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})
, we sampled
a^{\prime}\sim\pi(\cdot|s^{\prime})
instead of making use of
\pi(a^{\prime}|s^{\prime})
for all
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
in every method, for the sake of computational convenience.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}\bigg\{\log f(Z^{\prime}|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a))\bigg\}\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{M}w_{j_{2}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1})\cdot\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\log\bigg\{\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{M}w_{j_{1}}(s,a;\theta)\cdot\phi(z_{j_{2},b}^{\prime}\ ;\ \mu_{j_{1}}(s,a;\theta),\ \sigma_{j_{1}}^{2}(s,a;\theta))\bigg\}.

For Hyvarinen divergence, we instead maximized Hyvarinen score with individual term being
\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}\{S_{H}\big(Z^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a)\big)\}
as follows, which is computed by MC approximation in the same way (assuming GMM for
P
),

\displaystyle S_{H}(z,P):=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\log f(z|P)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\log f(z|P)\bigg)^{2}=

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{M}w_{j}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{(z-\mu_{j})^{2}}{\sigma_{j}^{4}}-\frac{1}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\bigg\}\cdot\phi(z;\mu_{j};\sigma_{j}^{2})}{\sum_{j=1}^{M}w_{j}\cdot\phi(z;\mu_{j},\sigma_{j}^{2})}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bigg\{\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{M}w_{j}\cdot\big(\frac{-(z-\mu_{j})}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\big)\cdot\phi(z;\mu_{j},\sigma_{j}^{2})}{\sum_{j=1}^{M}w_{j}\cdot\phi(z;\mu_{j},\sigma_{j}^{2})}\bigg\}^{2}.

For TVD, we approximated
\|f(\cdot|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s_{i},a_{i}))-f(\cdot|\Psi_{\pi}(r_{i},s_{i}^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}},a_{i}^{\prime}))\|_{L_{1}}
by using the density ratio as follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z^{\prime}\sim\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}})}\bigg\{\bigg|1-\frac{f(Z^{\prime}|\Upsilon_{\theta}(s,a))}{f(Z^{\prime}|\Psi_{\pi}(r,s^{\prime},\Upsilon_{\hat{\theta}_{n,t-1}}))}\bigg|\bigg\}=\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{M}w_{j_{2}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1})\times

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}\bigg|1-\frac{\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{M}w_{j_{1}}(s,a;\theta)\cdot\phi(z_{j_{2},b}^{\prime}|\mu_{j_{1}}(s,a;\theta),\sigma_{j_{1}}^{2}(s,a;\theta))}{\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{M}w_{j_{2}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1})\cdot\phi(z_{j_{2},b}^{\prime}|r+\gamma\cdot\mu_{j_{2}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1}),\gamma^{2}\cdot\sigma_{j_{2}}^{2}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime};\theta_{-1}))}\bigg|.

We let
B=100
and
B=50
for deterministic transition and stochastic transition, respectively, which we will explain in the following subsection (Appendix
E.2.4
). We added a term
{\epsilon_{\rm var}}=1.0
to the variance
\sigma_{j}^{2}(s,a;\theta)
in PDF-L2, KL, TVD, to prevent explosion of density values, thereby mitigating numerical instability, and used the same value for the tuning parameter of RBF FDE, i.e.,
{\sigma_{\rm RBF}}=1.0
. For Hyvarinen, we put
{\epsilon_{\rm var}}=10.0
since it was more prone to numerical instabilities. Even so, we could not run simulations for
M=200
due to numerical stabilities. The good thing about Energy FDE is that it does not require any no tuning parameter, which makes it more user-friendly (and performance was good as well).

E.2.4
Extended simulations

In our simulations, we always assumed
\gamma=0.95
. Every time a reward is observed, we clipped it between
-10
and
10
, and then multiplied it by a fixed constant (
20
in our case). Then, we tried two different settings: (i) deterministic transition
{\tilde{p}}(r,s^{\prime}|s,a)
as the original Atari games, (ii) added small noise
N(0,1)
to every reward to make it more random (which makes it a better environment to apply distributional RL). We also tried two behavior policies for each setting, which leads to different coverage. After repeating this for 5 different seeds, we plotted
(\text{Mean}\pm\text{STD})
area for each method.

First, let us start with Setting-1, deterministic transition. Here, the target policy is
\pi_{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}^{*}
(
{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}
-greedy version of DQN-trained optimal policy
\pi^{*}
) with
{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}=0.3
. The behavior policy is
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.4,\ 0.8
(but
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.4,\ 0.5
for Enduro and Pong to prevent sparse observation of rewards). Of course, weak coverage (i.e.,
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}
being a lot bigger than
{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}
) leads to worse performance. Results are visualized in the first two rows of Figures
3
–
5
for
N=10K
(Tables
5
–
18
for
N=2K,5K,10K
). In the simulations,
M
refers to the number of gaussian mixtures in GMM model and the number of quantiles in quantile-based models (QRDQN, IQN).

Second, we ran simulations on Setting-2, random transition (or reward). Here, the target policy is
\pi=\pi^{*}
(determnistic policy learned by DQN) in all games except Breakout. In Breakout, we let
\pi=\pi_{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}^{*}
with
{\epsilon_{\rm tar}}=0.3
to prevent the agent from being stuck at certain point (not proceeding with the game and repeating the same actions). The behavior policy has
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.1,\ 0.5
for all games (
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.1,\ 0.3
for Pong only). Results are visualized in the last two rows of Figures
3
–
5
for
N=10K
(Tables
19
–
32
for
N=2K,5K,10K
).

In many cases, we could see that the inaccuracy levels became lower with larger sample sizes, although there are exceptions as well, due to highly complex setting and model that we used. In general, we could see that KL showed the most accurate estimation. We could also see that density modeling (based on gaussian mixture models) helps improving the accuracy, even when the reward is deterministic. This can be seen by comparing our methods (KL, Energy, PDF-L2, RBF) with quantile-based methods (QRDQN, IQN) that use the same number of
M
(number of gaussian components in GMM or number of quantiles).

(Mean
\pm

-inaccuracy in each games (columns) for different methods with
M=10
,

: (row1) deterministic transition with strong coverage (
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.4
), (row2) deterministic transition with weak coverage (
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}>0.4
), (row3) random transition with strong coverage (
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}=0.1
), (row4) random transition with weak coverage (
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}>0.1
).

,

: (row1) deterministic transition with strong coverage (

), (row2) deterministic transition with weak coverage (

), (row3) random transition with strong coverage (

), (row4) random transition with weak coverage (
{\epsilon_{\rm beh}}>0.1
).

).

E.3
Computation resources

Our simulations are extensive. We used high performance computing system. For LQR simulation (Appendix
E.1
), we have used CPU, 2GB memory. A single run of a single method under a fixed setting takes approximately 20 seconds. For Atari games’ simulation (Appendix
E.2
), we have used GPU, 10GB memory. A single run of a single method under a fixed setting takes approximately 700 seconds.
